House Committee advances Strategic Bitcoin Reserve bill with narrow path to passage

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The current 119th Congress is expected to adjourn in the coming weeks, leaving little time for a House floor vote, Senate consideration, and final passage before the session ends.

The House Financial Services Committee has advanced H.R. 8957, the American Reserve Modernization Act of 2026, which would codify a Strategic Bitcoin Reserve and impose a minimum 20-year lock on federally held bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC).

The legislation, introduced on May 21 by Rep. Nick Begich, would allow the Treasury secretary to establish a Strategic Bitcoin Reserve and a separate Digital Asset Stockpile for other cryptocurrencies 180 days after enactment.

Once deposited in the reserve, bitcoin cannot be "sold, swapped, auctioned, encumbered, or otherwise disposed of for any purpose" for at least 20 years. The bill also includes transparency requirements such as proof-of-reserve reporting and directs federal agencies to report existing holdings.

On Sept. 16, the House Financial Services Committee approved it on a party-line vote of 28-21, with support from every Republican representative and opposition from every Democrat. On Nov. 24, the committee officially sent the revised bill to the full House, where it is now eligible for a floor vote.

The bill builds on U.S. President Donald Trump's executive order issued in March 2025 that directed the creation of a bitcoin reserve funded mainly through bitcoin already owned by the government from criminal or civil forfeitures, and a separate digital asset stockpile. The order sparked concern from some lawmakers on bitcoin's riskiness and volatility.

While the bill is now on the Union Calendar as Calendar No. 789, its chances of passing are low. The current 119th Congress is expected to adjourn in the coming weeks, leaving little time for a House floor vote, Senate consideration, and final passage before the session ends.

Polymarket odds show a 4% chance of the U.S. establishing a national bitcoin reserve by the end of this year, down from 60% around a year ago and 34% in May this year.

If the bill fails to pass within this Congress, it would have to be reintroduced in the 120th Congress. The Strategic Bitcoin Reserve created by the March 2025 executive order would remain in place, but a future president could alter or reverse it without congressional approval. Reintroducing and advancing the initiative may face steeper challenges as Democrats are set to control both chambers starting in January.

US announces new tariffs on host of countries including China

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Watch: What we know about Trump's Thanksgiving tariffs

The US has announced a fresh raft of tariffs as President Donald Trump pushes ahead with his signature trade policy.

The new taxes charged on imports to America are set to hit many of the country's biggest trading partners - China, the European Union and 12 other jurisdictions, including Japan, South Korea and Taiwan.

The White House began investigating the trade practices of other countries in March after the US Supreme Court ruled Trump's previously announced Liberation Day tariffs were illegal.

China is facing the highest tariff rate of up to 40%, followed by Vietnam at up to 35%, with others facing either 20% or 25%.

The US Trade Representative (USTR) announced the proposed tariffs together with extensive reports of how each jurisdiction's trade practices purportedly impact American commerce.

The new duties are not expected to take effect until next year and have to go through rounds of public consultation and, potentially, hearings.

Tariffs are taxes levied on imported goods. The amount is typically calculated as a percentage of a good's value and paid to the government by businesses importing products.

Some of these rates will be effectively reduced because of caps on tariffs Trump has agreed to. Individual countries also face different levies.

The proposed tariffs will affect goods from China, the European Union, Singapore, Switzerland, Indonesia, Malaysia, Cambodia, Thailand, Korea, Vietnam, Taiwan, Bangladesh, Japan, and India.

The USTR's Jamieson Greer said the latest tariffs issued would "level the playing field for American workers and chart a new, more sustainable course for the world economy".

He said that many of America's trading partners had "for decades" adopted industrial strategies which included "targeting manufacturing, wage and labor suppression".

They also employ "other market distorting practices that tilt the trade balance in their favour while eroding America's manufacturing base", Greer said.

But economists have warned tariffs can fuel inflation and push prices up for consumers if businesses pass on higher costs, as well as dampen global trade.

Trump has repeatedly threatened or imposed tariffs during his second term, arguing they will boost US manufacturing and create jobs.

Despite a poor result for the Republican party in the midterm elections, the president has continued to press on with his flagship trade policy.

Trump is also seeking to cut the US trade deficit - the gap between the value of goods it buys from other countries and those it sells to them.

Trump's so-called Liberation Day tariffs were struck down by the Supreme Court earlier this year

These new levies are build on a "stronger legal foundation" under US law compared with Trump's April 2025 Liberation Day tariffs, Singapore Management University economics lecturer Chang Pao-Li said.

Trump previously invoked emergency presidential powers to impose tariffs that have since been struck down, while his latest approach relies on the USTR's authority to impose tariffs to address certain foreign trade practices, she said.

The levies are still likely to face legal challenges, but there has been a "historical precedent" for such tariffs remaining in force, Chang said.

Many tariffs imposed under Section 301 - a law that allows the US to probe and retaliate against foreign trade practices - during Trump's first term remained in effect during his successor Joe Biden's administration.

However, Stephen Brown, chief North America economist for Capital Economics, said the latest tariff announcement was "not as alarming as it looks", due to the taxes "essentially replacing" previously announced tariffs made in the summer.

"The increase in the effective tariff rate looks set to be a touch lower than we've been assuming for our forecasts, although the new tariffs will still provide a marginal boost to prices," he said in a note.

The additional tariff burden is likely to be small because a limited number of goods are subject to the new duties, Brown added.

Jurisdictions affected by the new wave of tariffs have voiced their disapproval over the Trump administration's move.

Washington has also proposed a 10% tariff on goods from Brazil, as part of a separate investigation over what it says are "harmful trade practices" undercutting American workers.

Brazilian authorities expressed their "outrage" at the announcement, saying that the move is aimed at pressuring the government to change regulations around issues like digital trade.

The South American nation faces duties on finished aircraft and sugar sold to the US.

"Brazil will not yield to such threats," President Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva's office said.

The European Commission rejected the new US tariffs, saying it would investigate the reasons for bringing them in. It pointed out that tariffs are limited to the 15% previously agreed.

The latest tariff rate is "not consistent" with the reality of bilateral economic-trade cooperation between the US and Vietnam, Vietnamese Deputy Prime Minister Bui Thanh Son said.

The Japanese government said the US tariffs were "regrettable".

Trump tariffs on Asia: Which economies were hit hardest?

China's growth accelerates as AI boom helps boost exports

US trading partners have been "waiting for this shoe to drop" and have argued that they are being wrongly accused of producing too much, said the Asia Society Policy Institute's Wendy Cutler, a former senior US trade negotiator.

"Many believe that China, not them, should be the target for US action," she added.

Chinese Foreign Ministry spokesperson Guo Jiakun described the new tariffs as a form of "political manipulation", describing as "false" the US claim that China produces more than it can consume.

The tariff announcement is likely to increase tensions between the US and China, the world's two biggest economies, who remain locked in a trade dispute and the AI race.

The two countries have also been embroiled in a tit-for-tat tariffs war, which is currently on hold.

Despite last week's meeting between Trump and China's President Xi Jinping, the two sides made no announcement on the trade war truce, which is set to expire on 10 January.

Beijing has remained vague on how it will respond but for now seems "prepared to live with the new rates provided they don't exceed 20%", Cutler said.

Additional reporting by Leehyun Choi in Seoul

Sam Rockwell’s New Spy Thriller Officially Says Goodbye to 1,000 Theaters

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The last time Sam Rockwell worked with Martin McDonagh, he ended up winning an Academy Award for his performance in Three Billboards Outside Ebbing, Missouri, having previously worked with McDonagh on Seven Psychopaths. There were another nine Oscar nominations for McDonagh's work four years ago when he reunited Colin Farrell and Brendan Gleeson for The Banshees of Inisherin, and this year, it's the turn of Rockwell and John Malkovich to play two men whose relationship is explored through McDonagh's own distinctive brand of dark comedy. Unfortunately, if you've been planning to see the movie over Thanksgiving weekend, it's about to become considerably more difficult to find a theater showing it.

According to the latest theater count figures from The Numbers, McDonagh's Wild Horse Nine has officially lost 1,000 theaters heading into its fourth weekend of release, reducing its domestic theatrical footprint by exactly 50%. Having opened in 1,100 theaters on November 6, the movie expanded to 2,175 locations in its second weekend, but it will now be playing in 100 fewer theaters than it was on opening day.

Wild Horse Nine is alos nearing the $20 million milestone, having made $18.31 million domestically to date, with $2.46 million of that coming last weekend, when its grosses fell by 46%. The movie was averaging $1,231 per theater over the weekend, although that figure dropped to just $192 on Monday. With Thanksgiving bringing a particularly competitive weekend for theaters, McDonagh's movie has now lost more than 54% of its locations since the second weekend of its release.

McDonagh takes us to Chile in 1973, where two CIA agents are sent to Easter Island shortly before the military coup. For Malkovich's Chris, who's spending an increasing amount of time contemplating his own mortality, there's quite a bit of history between himself and Rockwell's Lee to reckon with, and things become rather more complicated for the two men when Chris befriends a pair of students on the island.

Written and directed by McDonagh, the movie features Malkovich (Con Air) as Chris Carlisle, Rockwell (Moon) as Lee Chesterfield, Steve Buscemi (Boardwalk Empire) as MJ, Mariana di Girolamo (Ema) as Monica, Ailín Salas (The German Doctor) as Alicia, Tom Waits (Seven Psychopaths) as Jim, and Parker Posey (The White Lotus) as Marge.

Wild Horse Nine is currently playing in theaters. Stay tuned to Collider for all the latest box office news and updates.

Acura Makes Japanese-Market Debut with the U.S.-Built Integra Type S

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Integra Type S is now for sale in Japan, making it the first Acura-branded vehicle to reach the Japanese market.

The 2026 Acura Integra Type S is officially for sale in Japan. That might not seem like big news, considering Honda Motor Company is headquartered in Japan, but the release marks the first Acura-branded vehicle to be exported to the Japanese market. While this is Acura’s first time in Japan, it’s strangely not the Integra’s, which spent a few decades wearing a Honda badge from 1985 to 2006.

Most Acuras are designed and built in the United States and sold throughout North America. The new Integras for sale in Japan are the same as those sold stateside, meaning they’ll be left-hand-drive anomalies in a predominantly right-hand-drive market.

See all results for new 2027 Acura Integra Type S for sale near 20147

That also means they’ll get the same 320-hp turbocharged 2.0-liter inline-four that we’re quite fond of in the United States. The front-wheel-drive sport compact is only offered with a six-speed manual transmission and features a limited-slip differential, Brembo brakes, and 19-inch wheels wrapped in summer tires. The whole package comes together to reach 60 mph in a brisk 5.1 seconds.

Honda builds every Integra Type S at its assembly plant in Marysville, Ohio, and the engines come from Honda’s Anna Engine Plant, also located in Ohio. Honda began exporting vehicles from Ohio to Japan in 1988 and has shipped nearly 300,000 Hondas to Japan since then.

Honda says the Teg’s introduction in Japan marks a strong global demand for an enthusiast-centered performance sedan. If the Type S is well-received in the Japanese market, it’s likely that more Acuras could start making their way to Japan in the future. Pricing and the exact number of Integra Type S models available in Japan haven’t been released yet, but considering they’re identical to the Type S models sold on our shores, they’ll likely come in around $56,000.

She now autocrosses a 1994 Miata with her dad, which she learned to drive the night before her first event. Microdrama Revenues Hit $20B, But Profits Outside China Still Weak – MPA Report

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The global microdrama business is booming, but outside of China, where ByteDance rules the roost, profits haven’t yet caught with revenues.

That’s according to a Media Partners Asia (MPA) report, which reveals revenues on a global basis are estimated to rise 24% year-on-year to hit $20B in 2026, and that will hit $32B by 2031, a growth rate of 9.8% per year, but profitability is still thin.

The MPA’s second annual Micro-Drama Economy report notes that outside of China, where microdramas first emerged, every 56 cents in a dollar are spent on marketing and another 18 cents goes to app stores and payment providers, which is significantly reducing earnings. Related Stories News Dogwoof Moves Into Microdrama Game With Tattle TV Deal News Women In Vertical Media: Execs From Shorties Studios, aTwist & Eris Join Inaugural Advisory Circle

Excluding China, the microdrama biz is worth $4.87B, with the U.S. the largest at $1.71B, but has fallen 2% year-over-year and will be overtaken by Asia Pacific (excluding China) by 2029. The overall revenue figure will grow to $11B in 2031. Watch on Deadline

The MPA noted the two biggest microdrama groups take revenues of more than $1B a year, but within that DramaBox recorded a net margin of 3.2% in 2025 and ReelShort lost $12M, though it made an $8M profit the first quarter of this year.

DramaBox and GoodShort, which announced a merger at the end of October, along with ReelShort, Kunlun and NetShort collectively account for 65% of revenues outside of China, where the real action is right now. All four apps are Chinese-owned or backed. India is providing the fastest growth, with revenues tripling this year, as local platforms Kuku TV and Story TV boomed and JioStar debuted in the space in April with JioHotstar service Tadka. India is also the largest Asian market outside China with $322M.

MPA’s assessment is that the microdrama business outside of China is not yet settled on a model, but TikTok is predicted to be a dominant force, as it is “positioned on every side” – selling apps’ advertising, carrying their series and, through its free app PineDrama, competing with them.

TikTok’s parent, ByteDance is applying the model that shaped the Chinese market – using free, advertising-funded apps to gain audiences first and monetizing later – and MPA predicts it will gain “significant scale in both the U.S.” and Southeast Asia by 2031. Of course, ByteDance was forced to sell a majority of its shares in TikTok in the U.S., but does retain a minority stake.

YouTube and Meta are not expected to make a major impression on the microdrama space, while locally the likes of Vigloo in Korea and Fuji TV’s Fod Short in Japan are competing with Chinese-backed apps.

China accounts for 76% of total microdrama revenues ($15.2B), following 90% growth in 2025. This will slow to 6.7% in 2031 as viewing plateaus. The MPA doesn’t estimate profits in China as none of the leading players such as top trio ByteDance, Tencent and Kuaishou single microdrama out in their financing reporting.

Advertising on free services took over paid viewing last year, and this year accounts for 69% of revenues, That will grow to 79% in 2031. ByteDance, which operates Hongguo and Douyin, earning 59% of China’s revenues. ByteDance, Tencent and Kuaishou collectively account for 82% of the market.

MPA calculates revenue as what viewers, advertisers and brands pay before app-store and payment fees; and outside China it covers dedicated apps and their web stores, not general social feeds. In China, MPA’s estimates exclude producers’ spending on AI models and platform investment, which are both included in the National Radio and Television Administration’s RMB100B estimate for 2025.

“Demand for micro drama is established in every major market, but outside China this is still an emerging business, and it has yet to show that it can earn a durable profit,” said Vivek Couto, CEO and Executive Director of MPA.

“The leading apps spend more than half of their revenue to acquire viewers, and most of those viewers do not pay. Consolidation has begun, and China has already moved to free, advertising-funded viewing. The companies that endure will be those that retain their viewers, bill them directly and invest in titles worth returning to.”

The MPA report raised a further notable asides: new title listings on apps outside China rose from about 100,000 in 2025 to 440,000 in 2026, and 75% were made with AI. This counts series that are made in different languages more than once. MPA’s research shows an AI-made title costs “about a tenth of a series filmed in Los Angeles,” it added.

“AI has made a title cheap enough to test almost any idea, and catalogues have filled accordingly,” said Myat Pan Phyu (May), Lead Analyst, Micro Drama and Vertical Video for MPA. “Viewing has moved far less: live action still dominates the leading titles on most of the platforms we track.

“What decides a platform’s economics is how many of its titles earn back the marketing spent on them. That favours companies with established IP, stars and audiences of their own, which is why local broadcasters, studios and telecom operators matter to the next phase.”

Microdrama Revenue to Hit $20 Billion in 2026, but Profits Outside China Remain Thin, Media Partners Asia Report Finds

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Global microdrama revenue will climb 24% to $20 billion in 2026, but outside China the business has yet to prove it can make reliable money, according to new research from Media Partners Asia.

The Asia-focused research and advisory firm’s second annual study of the sector, “The Microdrama Economy 2026,” projects the market will reach $32 billion by 2031, on annual growth of 9.8%. MPA’s figures measure spending by viewers, advertisers and brands before app stores and payment processors take their cut. Outside China, they count only dedicated microdrama apps and their web storefronts, not general social media feeds. Related Stories Rock & Roll Hall of Fame Designates the Troubadour as a Landmark Venue; Going Forward, L.A. Will Host Hall's Induction Ceremony 'At Least Every Other Year' (EXCLUSIVE) Phil Collins Says He’s a ‘Well Man’ Now and Hints at the ‘Possibility of Doing Some Shows’ Amid Health Battle: 'You Haven't Heard the Last of Me'

China still dominates. The country will generate $15.2 billion this year, or 76% of the global total, after revenue jumped 90% in 2025. MPA expects growth there to cool to 6.7% annually as viewing levels off, taking Chinese revenue to $21 billion by 2031. The firm’s China figure is narrower than the National Radio and Television Administration’s RMB100 billion ($15 billion) estimate for 2025, which also counts producer outlays on AI models and platform investment.

Free, ad-supported viewing overtook paid viewing in China in 2025. Advertising will make up 69% of the country’s microdrama revenue this year and 79% by 2031. ByteDance, which operates Hongguo and Douyin, takes 56% of Chinese revenue, and ByteDance, Tencent and Kuaishou together command 82%.

Dedicated apps outside China will earn $4.87 billion in 2026, a third more than last year, with MPA forecasting $11 billion by 2031 on annual growth of 17.7%. The U.S. remains the biggest single market at $1.71 billion, though that is 2% lower than in 2025. Asia-Pacific excluding China, at $1.44 billion, is set to overtake the U.S. in 2029.

India, at $322 million, now outranks Japan and Korea as the biggest market in Asia after China. It is also the fastest-growing, with revenue more than tripling this year. Local players Kuku TV and Story TV both roughly tripled their takings, and JioStar launched its microdrama service Tadka on JioHotstar in April. India is the only big market where local platforms lead, selling cheap subscriptions that bypass app-store billing. JioStar’s existing streaming audience has reduced its need to pay for new users, and the company is now turning to retention and growing ad revenue.

Revenue is concentrated. ReelShort, DramaBox–GoodShort, Kunlun and NetShort together generate 65% of the total outside China, and each is either owned or financed from China. DramaBox and GoodShort announced a merger on Oct. 31, the first such deal among the sector’s top players, partly so the two would stop outbidding one another for advertising. Fox, Versant and NBCUniversal are among the U.S. media companies that have bought minority stakes in the sector, while India’s and Korea’s biggest local platforms are gearing up to go public.

Some homegrown companies take on the Chinese-owned apps directly, among them Korea’s Vigloo and Fuji TV’s FOD Short in Japan. MPA sees their edge in what the Chinese apps don’t have: built-in audiences, homegrown IP and talent, and a way to bill viewers without payment cards. Japanese and Korean broadcasters, studios and telcos mostly work as producers. Across Southeast Asia, telcos have sold heavily discounted passes to build reach, though MPA said it is unclear whether these bring in new paying customers or simply shift existing ones to a cheaper channel.

TikTok sits on all sides of the market. It sells advertising to the apps, carries their series and competes with them through its free app PineDrama. In Southeast Asia, ByteDance is repeating its China playbook, building audiences with free, ad-funded apps before cashing in. MPA expects TikTok to become a major player in the U.S. and Southeast Asia alike by 2031. YouTube stays open to creators and studios, while Meta mainly serves as the place where apps find new users. Even so, MPA doesn’t expect free platforms to push out the paid apps, which control the IP and the billing relationship with paying customers.

Profits are the sticking point. For each dollar of revenue generated outside China, 56 cents goes to marketing and another 18 cents to app stores and payment processors. In a typical month, just one in 12 users or so pays anything. The two largest groups each bring in more than $1 billion a year, yet DramaBox posted a 3.2% net margin in fiscal 2025. ReelShort lost $12 million in 2025 before swinging to a profit of roughly $8 million in the January-March quarter of 2026.

“Demand for microdrama is established in every major market, but outside China this is still an emerging business, and it has yet to show that it can earn a durable profit,” said Vivek Couto, CEO and executive director of Media Partners Asia. “The leading apps spend more than half of their revenue to acquire viewers, and most of those viewers do not pay. Consolidation has begun, and China has already moved to free, advertising-funded viewing. The companies that endure will be those that retain their viewers, bill them directly and invest in titles worth returning to.”

AI is reshaping supply far faster than demand. By MPA’s count, new titles listed on non-China apps will surge from around 100,000 in 2025 to 440,000 this year, with the AI-generated share climbing from 15% to 75%. Each listing represents a single title in a single language on a single app, so a series that runs in multiple languages or across multiple apps shows up more than once. Producing a title with AI costs roughly 10% of what a Los Angeles shoot would.

Audiences have been slower to switch. Live-action titles hold most of the top 50 spots on five of the six non-China platforms MPA tracks. In China, about nine in 10 titles released in 2026 are AI-made, but they bring in just 20% of the year’s revenue.

“What decides a platform’s economics is how many of its titles earn back the marketing spent on them,” said Myat Pan Phyu, MPA’s lead analyst for microdrama and vertical video. “That favors companies with established IP, stars and audiences of their own, which is why local broadcasters, studios and telecom operators matter to the next phase.”

Payments may shift the math. Apple and Google process 65% of the revenue earned outside China, though legal rulings and fresh legislation in the U.S., Japan and Korea have cleared the way for web payments. Direct billing can lift margins, although the savings depend on each market’s fees and payment route. MPA expects payments made outside the app stores, including through India’s UPI system, to deliver roughly half of the growth outside China in 2027.

Originally created by engineer Josh Wardle as a gift for his partner, Wordle rapidly spread to become an international phenomenon, with thousands of people around the globe playing every day. Today's NYT Strands hints will be easy if you're a good friend.

Strands, the New York Times' elevated word-search game, requires the player to perform a twist on the classic word search. Words can be made from linked letters — up, down, left, right, or diagonal, but words can also change direction, resulting in quirky shapes and patterns. Every single letter in the grid will be part of an answer. There's always a theme linking every solution, along with the "spangram," a special, word or phrase that sums up that day's theme, and spans the entire grid horizontally or vertically.

By providing an opaque hint and not providing the word list, Strands creates a brain-teasing game that takes a little longer to play than its other games, like Wordle and Connections.

Here's a roadmap to the Aaron Rodgers developments, one of the craziest stories in years

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If you were traveling on Wednesday or otherwise not plugged in for one of the most rollicking, unpredictable days in years if not ever, you’re in luck.

We’ve reconstructed the full timeline as to the events regarding now-former Steelers quarterback Aaron Rodgers.

It actually started on Monday, when coach Mike McCarthy was asked whether he has considered using one of the team’s other three quarterbacks when the offense is struggling. McCarthy, whose body language in hindsight suggests he was dreading the question, said without equivocation, “Aaron Rodgers is our quarterback.”

On Tuesday, Rodgers took his usual reps at practice. He conducted his usual midweek press conference. Unbeknownst to most of the media or the general public, the Steelers were changing their play-calling procedures to have quarterbacks coach Tom Arth, and not McCarthy, communicate the calls to Rodgers through the speaker in his helmet.

Then came Tuesday night. At 11:10 p.m. ET, the Steelers issued a statement — not from coach Mike McCarthy or G.M. Omar Khan or owner Art Rooney II but from team spokesman Burt Lauten — that Mason Rudolph will start on Friday against the Broncos and Rodgers will be inactive.

The news forced the Broncos to make a quick pivot in their preparations from Rodgers to Rudolph. As one source put it, the Broncos weren’t sure whether it was a good thing or a bad thing.

It was definitely a bad thing, all things considered, for the Steelers.

By Wednesday morning, reports emerged that it was a coach’s decision. At 8:00 a.m. ET, McCarthy informed the team of the decision.

Rodgers wasn’t there. As it turns out, he was excused.

Enter Pat McAfee. He teased a surprise guest at 1:40 p.m. ET. As expected, it was Rodgers.

Rodgers took a flamethrower to McCarthy, airing out dirty laundry that created a major mess for his now-former team. He exposed the fact that, at halftime of the Week 11 loss to the Eagles, McCarthy broke down the locker-room gathering by saying, “1-2-3 Packers.”

Rodgers also said McCarthy was falling asleep during some meetings and missing other meetings, expressing (in passive-aggressive fashion) concern for McCarthy’s health.

But Rodgers also said it was nothing new, and that McCarthy had done the same things in Green Bay, when they worked there together a decade ago.

Rodgers also made it clear he wouldn’t stay with the Steelers. After his session with McAfee and company, Dulac reported that the Steelers were expected to release Rodgers. By the official close of business, they’d waived him.

A question lingers as to whether the Steelers will try to deny Rodgers the balance of his $22 million salary. It won’t be easy; they’ll need to both void the guarantees and wipe out his eligibility for termination pay (in the form of his remaining salary) under the Collective Bargaining Agreement.

McCarthy met with reporters on Wednesday, after all hell had broken loose. He admitted to the “1-2-3 Packers” comment, calling it an “[e]mbarrassing moment.”

McCarthy also said that Rodgers was benched after issuing an “ultimatum” that was “clearly outside of his job description.”

ESPNFL thereafter reported that McCarthy “has the full support” of the organization, and that the team is “not planning to make any immediate changes.” The key word may be “immediate"; with few viable options only two days before a Black Friday game against the Broncos, changes could come later.

The overriding takeaway is that someone with the Steelers knew or should have known that McCarthy and Rodgers couldn’t co-exist. Because, obviously, they didn’t co-exist.

If, as Rodgers claims, McCarthy believed Rodgers got him fired in Green Bay, McCarthy shouldn’t have wanted a reunion with Rodgers. And if, as Rodgers claims, McCarthy was doing the same kinds of things in Green Bay that he did in Pittsburgh, Rodgers shouldn’t have wanted a reunion with McCarthy.

The reunion has now ended abruptly, with seven games left in the 2026 regular season. It remains to be seen whether Rodgers wants to play with another team, or whether another team wants him. Any team that signs him risks being on the receiving end of a future gripe session from Rodgers in which he lists all the things that team and/or the coach did wrong — without ever acknowledging his own role in the reality that it didn’t work out.

The good news, if there is any, is this: If Rodgers never plays again, his final NFL pass will have been a touchdown.

Which is a better outcome than if he’d retired after the 2025 season. His final pass that year was a pick-six in the playoffs.

S&P warns Nvidia credit rating could be cut over 'rising' management and governance risk

📅 2026-11-26  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Nvidia's credit rating could be cut by one notch over the next 90 days if the company continues to have "deficiencies" in its management and governance framework, S&P Global Ratings said in the wake of the Jensen Huang's ouster and reinstatement as Nvidia CEO.

"News regarding the CEO and questions regarding potential export control violations have exposed vulnerabilities in Nvidia's management and governance framework, especially in regard to its board structure, composition, and effectiveness," S&P said in a Wednesday note.

Nvidia currently holds an AA rating, the third-highest level.

S&P has placed the chipmaker on "CreditWatch with negative implications," though any potential downgrade would be limited to one notch, to AA-, unless investigations or disclosures lead the agency to conclude that Nvidia's reputation or future prospects have also been harmed.

A downgrade can pressure bond prices, push yields higher and raise borrowing costs. Nvidia priced $25 billion of investment-grade senior notes in June, with maturities from 2028 through 2056, shortly after S&P upgraded the company from AA- to AA.

Nvidia has about $32.5 billion of bonds in the U.S. high-grade corporate bond index, according to Bloomberg.

S&P's Wednesday note follows Moody's Ratings changing its outlook on Nvidia to stable from positive on Nov. 18, citing governance concerns, and leaving that outlook unchanged after Huang's return on Monday.

In its note, S&P cited the "long tenure" of certain board members as a reason their independence may be "compromised" and said Nvidia's management and governance risk was "rising."

The ratings agency added that it remained unaware of any remedial action taken by the board to address what could be "meaningful" management and governance deficiencies.

Chairman Harvey Jones and early investor Tench Coxe have served on Nvidia's board since 1993, while Brooke Seawell joined in 1997. Mark Stevens served from 1993 to 2006 and rejoined the board in 2008.

S&P also flagged questions around geopolitical risk management and internal controls arising from Nvidia's exposure to China.

"While Nvidia management has delivered significant returns for its stakeholders over decades, weak executive oversight and lack of succession planning have brought questions around the "key man" risk to the forefront," S&P's Andrew Chang and David Tsui said.

Chang and Tsui noted that Huang's resignation and subsequent reinstatement did not resolve questions about the board's oversight of executive conduct.

The agency also intends to meet with Nvidia's board and management to discuss plans to address those deficiencies, particularly around "board independence, effective management oversight and a credible leadership succession plan."

Huang returned less than two weeks after Jones said Nvidia's directors did not intend to reinstate him. The board later voted unanimously to bring Huang back, sending Nvidia shares up as much as 36% during Monday's session.

Nvidia shares are down about 12.5% since Monday's close.

8 Best Steamy Shows for Adults Only You Can Stream Now

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

There are some TV shows that are immediate guilty pleasures because of the type of content they present. It’s not that we feel guilty because they’re bad, but because we blush when we tell our friends and family about them. And yes, they're all pretty steamy! There’s nothing wrong with them; they’re just not safe for work! We’re here to celebrate eight steamy shows you can stream right now! And fret not, you’re not going to be alone!

From a gay hockey show to a period drama where love and marriage always involve intimacy, these eight shows are sex-positive, romantically-tinged, and genuinely juicy! These shows are so steamy, you might need to hide under the covers or shield your eyes! But the brilliance of these eight shows is that they’re more than just steamy; they have excellent storylines, thrills, shocks, and brilliant characters. While there are certainly many other shows that can be added to this roster, this smattering gives you a range of options from various genres and eras.

If you love cheap thrills, 56 Days is for you! Developed by Karyn Usher and Lisa Zwerling, and based on Catherine Ryan Howard's novel, the eight-episode erotic psychological thriller centers around Ciara Wyse (Dove Cameron) and wealthy architect Oliver Kennedy (Avan Jogia), who dive into an intense romance after meeting at a supermarket. Exactly 56 days later, an unidentifiable, decomposing body is discovered in the bathtub of Oliver’s luxury Boston apartment. Moving back and forth across their timeline, the series chronicles detectives Lee Reardon (Karla Souza) and Karl Connolly (Dorian Missick) as they investigate the murder and untangle the dark secrets both lovers are hiding. A bingeworthy mystery, 56 Days leads with a whodunnit hook before delivering an intense, steamy, sex-centric relationship thriller.

The story opens on Day 56, leading to an unrelenting mystery where the central suspects are all but reliable sources. Both leads harbor hidden motives and trust issues, creating tension over who is manipulating whom as their whirlwind relationship escalates. Yet through their individual missions, their physical relationship remains the only authentic thing. It’s a fast-burning relationship that is rooted in an intense, lust-driven affair. The narrative embraces high-gloss melodrama, dark secrets, and explicit adult content, prioritizing Ciara and Oliver’s bedroom scenes and sensationalism not to be provocative but to display the spectrum of their relationship. It’s unhinged and messy, but he’s delicious to watch from an outside perspective. 56 Days is an addictive premise that keeps you hooked until the bitter end.

Since Season 1, Bridgerton has gotten fans all hot and bothered because period romance is super steamy! The Shondaland drama based on Julia Quinn's book series follows the eight close-knit siblings of the powerful Bridgerton family as they navigate high society's competitive marriage market in search of true love. The Regency-era alternate history spotlights a different sibling's romantic journey amidst lavish balls, family duty, and social expectations. Along the way, the elite members of the "ton" are scrutinized by the anonymous gossip columnist Lady Whistledown (Julie Andrews), whose scandal sheets expose their deepest secrets and fuel society-wide intrigue. A beautifully shot series that blends extraordinary costumes, immaculate set pieces, and a modern pop soundtrack covered by a classical string quartet, Bridgerton is truly all about the intimacy. And the show never falls short of that fantasy.

Bridgerton emerged as a remarkably beloved series because it pairs explicit, emotionally charged intimacy with high-stakes era Romance told through modern pacing. Created by Chris Van Dusen, Bridgerton is heavy on the romance, scandal, and family dynamics, but when the season's lovers find their moments together, that's when Bridgerton truly heats up. Intimate scenes drive character arcs rather than serving as mere shock value. Whether it be sexual awakenings, intimate vulnerability, or shifting power dynamics built within the boudoir, these moments are genuine narrative moments that are earned. Each season builds longing through classic romance setups, from fake dating to enemies-to-lovers narratives, that make the payoff feel earned. With strict Regency social rules in mind, Bridgerton leads with reality, employing brilliant intimacy coordinators to choreograph the moments that emphasize emotional connection, consent, and the female gaze. Perhaps the only downside to these steamy scenes is the expectations they establish in our world! Damn you, Simon Basset (Regé-Jean Page)!

Four Netflix characters, four completely different ways of showing up for somebody. This is the one who fits how you actually do friendship, and what having them in your life would be like.

You do not do friendship at half volume, and neither does he. What you want is somebody who says yes to the plan before you have finished describing the plan.

You are the friend who makes things happen, and so is she. Together you would be mildly exhausting to everyone around you and completely delighted with yourselves.

You have no patience for pleasant lies, and she has no ability to tell them. What looks like coldness from the outside is the most reliable friendship on this list.

You look after whoever is having the worst day. So does he. It is the least strategic way to move through the world and the best possible quality in a friend.

Perhaps one of the most important queer stories ever told, Fellow Travelers, based on the Thomas Mallon novel, is an emotionally charged, decades-spanning romance. The romantic thriller chronicles the secret love affair between two very different men, State Department official Hawkins Fuller (Matt Bomer) and idealistic congressional staffer Tim Laughlin (Jonathan Bailey), who meet in 1950s Washington, D.C. Beginning at the height of the McCarthy-era Lavender Scare in which the government actively hunted down and purged gay employees, Hawkins and Tim's clandestine relationship is tested by political paranoia, personal betrayal, and societal bigotry. With sweeping historical scope, Fellow Travelers preserves a crucial moment in history through explicit, frank intimacy driven by repressed desire, shifting power dynamics, and high-stakes historical danger.

Created by Ron Nyswaner, Fellow Travelers is a brilliant character-driven drama. The series does a stand-up job at presenting fully-fleshed individuals who must balance their personal desires with a fight to survive. These characters must guard every word and gesture in public. But then, behind closed doors, physical passion acts as an urgent release, heightening the power of intimacy. These moments range from tenderness, roughness, control, and surrender. As out actors, Bomer and Bailey have remarkable chemistry, understanding the gravity of these moments between Hawkins and Tim. While some scenes may be graphic, they're balanced with truth. These scenes will certainly make you blush, but they'll also hit your heart hard.

We're going to the cottage! Very few shows took the world by storm quite like Heated Rivalry. Created by Jacob Tierney and based on Rachel Reid's Game Changers book series, the Canadian romance series follows rival hockey superstars, Shane Hollander (Hudson Williams) and Ilya Rozanov (Connor Storrie). Over an eight-year span, the opposing captains engage in a secret, passionate romantic relationship off the ice while maintaining a fierce public rivalry on it. Together, they navigate intense professional pressures, family expectations, and the personal risks of keeping their love hidden in the high-stakes world of professional sports. Heated Rivalry examines toxic masculinity and the pressures of staying closeted in athletics while celebrating themes of identity, queer joy, and acceptance.

Heated Rivalry emerged as a breakout hit because it immediately dove deep into the intimacy. It pairs explicit, consent-driven romance with high-stakes tension and emotional vulnerability. Physical encounters act as an early language between Shane and Ilya while they struggle with verbal honesty and public expectations. As their bond shifts over time, so does their ability to communicate their true feelings. The scenes, staged by intimacy coordinator Chala Hunter, feel lived-in, playful, and emotionally grounded, balancing humor, awkwardness, and tenderness. Rather than lean into tragic tropes where intimacy leads to ruin, the narrative highlights the impact of true love, mutual respect, and emotional payoff. Beyond Shane and Ilya, Heated Rivalry crafts their own rivalry as the beautiful relationship between Scott Hunter (François Arnaud) and Kip Grady (Robbie G.K.) showcases a very different connection. Heated Rivalry became an instant obsession because the love is real. Come for the smut, stay for the love!

Based on the book series by Diana Gabaldon, the historical fantasy series Outlander is quite a compelling story. Developed by Ronald D. Moore, Outlander follows Claire Randall (Caitríona Balfe), a British World War II combat nurse who is mysteriously swept back in time from 1945 to 1743 Scotland. Forced to marry a chivalrous Highland warrior named Jamie Fraser (Sam Heughan) for protection, she falls deeply in love with him as she navigates a dangerous world. Torn between two eras and two men, Claire uses her modern medical skills to survive as she and Jamie become entangled in major historical upheavals, from the Jacobite Rising to the American Revolution. Outlander blends a time-travel mystery, historical drama, and an intense romance rooted in high-stakes survival with rich period pieces.

Outlander appealed to viewers looking for multi-genre drama handled with care. The eight-season series flourishes with its emotional depth and steamy romance, but it also does feature significant graphic violence, explicit torture, and sexual assault scenes. That said, those moments are counterbalanced by its intimacy. It focuses on desire and partnership. Claire openly initiates and enjoys sexual moments, while Jamie's devotion and low ego put his partner's feelings first. As Jamie and Claire grow older and face major trials and tribulations, the intimacy shifts from early, urgent passion to deeper trust and emotional understanding. With time travel, clan politics, and a lush Scottish locale setting the scene, Outlander masters heightened intimate tension in every moment the pair get together.

Inspired by 44 Chapters About 4 Men by BB Easton, the two-season erotic drama Sex/Life follows Billie Connelly (Sarah Shahi), a suburban wife and mother of two, who feels unfulfilled by the fading passion in her stable marriage to her husband, Cooper (Mike Vogel). Torn between her domestic life and memories of her wild past in New York City, Billie begins documenting her explicit fantasies about her bad-boy ex-boyfriend, Brad Simon (Adam Demos), in a private journey. When Cooper discovers the diary and Brad re-enters her life, Billie's past and present collide in a complicated love triangle. Sex/Life is a perfect series for those who love high-stakes melodrama, intense chemistry, and bold explorations of desire, identity, and marital complacency.

Sex/Life leads with a bold and provocative tone. With frequent nudity, candid conversations about desire, and steamy scenes until your heart's content, Sex/Life leans heavily into the crossroads of adult romance and soap opera intrigue. Beyond the fantasy elements, the series touches on losing the spark in long-term partnerships and balancing personal desires with necessary responsibilities. Created by Stacy Rukeyser, the show uses sex scenes to reflect shifting dynamics with Billie's "85/15" relationship tension, tapping into the friction between security and wilder impulses. At only 14 episodes, Sex/Life is a highly addictive, bingeable drama.

No show is as addictively frustrating as Tell Me Lies. Created by Meaghan Oppenheimer and based on the Carola Lovering novel, the three-season series follows the tumultuous, eight-year relationship between Lucy Albright (Grace Van Patten) and Stephen DeMarco (Jackson White) after they meet at Baird College. What starts as a typical campus romance quickly spirals into addiction, deceit, and emotional dependency. Jumping between their college years and the 2015 wedding of their friends Bree (Catherine Missal) and Evan (Brandon Cook), Tell Me Lies explores how Lucy and Stephen's destructive bond and dark secrets permanently alter the lives of everyone in their friend group. Tell Me Lies is a character-driven drama that leans heavily into the honest, messy relationships based on toxic pairings and psychological manipulation where sex is front and center.

While Tell Me Lies is filled to the brim with campus drama and steamy scenes, it's rooted in real emotional vulnerabilities. The show looks beyond surface-level romance to examine why people surrender their truth and self-respect to belong. The sexual scenes are presented as purpose-driven storytelling moments. Intimate scenes often highlight power shifts, emotional dependency, or shifting relationship dynamics rather than serving purely as filler fodder. Rather than reducing characters to simple heroes or villains, Tell Me Lies tackles insecurity, avoidance, and how small choices reshape an individual's personality and identity. The action escalates across each season, culminating in a shockingly obvious conclusion. If there’s one thing to take away from Tell Me Lies, it’s this: do not be like these people. None of them. They may have a redeemable quality or two, but they all display moments of manipulation that drop them into dangerous territory.

At the forefront of the early 2000s vampire obsession, True Blood was one of HBO's early smut spectacles of its heyday. The fantasy horror drama from Alan Ball follows Sookie Stackhouse (Anna Paquin), a telepathic waitress in the small town of Bon Temps, Louisiana, in a world where the invention of synthetic blood allows vampires to coexist publicly with humans. When Sookie falls in love with a mysterious 174-year-old vampire named Bill Compton (Stephen Moyer), her quiet life is upended as she is pulled into a dangerous underworld of vampire politics, werewolves, witches, and fairies. Blending dark romance, mystery, and Southern Gothic horror, True Blood explores the violent social clashes between humans and supernatural beings over assimilation and equal rights. Based on Charlaine Harris' The Southern Vampire Mysteries, True Blood balances pulpy escapism with masterful worldbuilding and distinct character development.

True Blood is the perfect balance of camp, romance, and grit. It shifts between murder mysteries, romantic entanglements, heavy gore, and self-aware humor without treating its melodrama as a distraction. True Blood leans into unapologetic sensuality, where desire is central to the plot. Vampire blood acts as both sustenance and an intoxicating, sensory trigger that blurs pain, pleasure, and power. It's the rush of intimate ecstasy that turns romance into a thrill ride. The story uses its setting's heat, humidity, and secrets to heighten the tension. At the same time, the concept of the "vampires out of the coffin" premise serves as a sharp debate on social and queer themes. From Sookie and Eric Northman's (Alexander Skarsgård) rival-to-romance tension-filled chemistry to Jason Stackhouse (Ryan Kwanten) and Jessica Hamby's (Deborah Ann Woll) chaotic, impulsive hookups, True Blood is never short of sex and intimacy. Even supernatural dream sequences are sexy spectacles! True Blood is not a show to watch with your parents around, no matter your age!

‘Baahubali,’ ‘RRR’ Tech Chief C.V. Rao, New SMPTE Fellow, on Why Indian Filmmakers Shouldn’t Depend on Overseas Facilities

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

C.V. Rao, who helped shape the production and post-production pipelines behind S.S. Rajamouli‘s “Baahubali” films and “RRR,” has become the first technologist from South India to be named a Fellow of the Society of Motion Picture and Television Engineers (SMPTE).

Rao, chief technology officer at Hyderabad-based Annapurna Studios, describes the honor as one that comes with added responsibility, along with a chance to learn from senior figures across the global SMPTE community. Related Stories Rock & Roll Hall of Fame Ceremony Gets Fired Up With Live-Wire Numbers From U2, the Chili Peppers, Usher, Cynthia Erivo, Sienna Spiro, Aerosmith and More Rock & Roll Hall of Fame Designates the Troubadour as a Landmark Venue; Going Forward, L.A. Will Host Hall's Induction Ceremony 'At Least Every Other Year' (EXCLUSIVE)

“It also reflects the tremendous technical talent available in India,” Rao tells Variety. “We have developed strong capabilities in adopting and implementing advanced filmmaking technologies, and we believe the next opportunity is to contribute more actively to innovation, technology development and international standards.”

“Baahubali” brought challenges well beyond what the studio had expected. The scale of the production demanded major upgrades in grading, processing power, storage, bandwidth and technical staff. For “Baahubali 2,” the team set up a dedicated peer-to-peer link between Hyderabad and Los Angeles to send large volumes of material straight to Imax. The experience also led to the launch of ANR Sound & Vision, a one-stop post facility that takes projects from data management and editing through VFX, sound, grading and final mastering.

The Dolby Cinema version of “RRR” had its roots in Las Vegas, where a Dolby Cinema demo at NAB led to talks with cinematographer K.K. Senthil Kumar, who pitched the idea to Rajamouli.

“We had to travel to Germany for the Dolby Cinema processing of ‘RRR,’ which raised an important question: why should Indian filmmakers depend on overseas facilities for these capabilities?” Rao says. “That experience became a major motivation for establishing Dolby Cinema processing capabilities at Annapurna Studios.”

Rao points out that India had skilled VFX artists and capable post houses well before either film. What Rajamouli’s productions brought was a new order of scale and complexity, which pushed studios to grow their capabilities, strengthen their teams and get departments working together more closely. Both films also relied on international partners. Attempting projects of that size entirely within India 10 years earlier would have been far tougher, he says, given the gaps in specialized infrastructure, processing capacity and experience at the time.

“The most significant impact was the confidence these projects created across the industry,” Rao says. “They demonstrated that Indian studios could handle increasingly complex work and encouraged greater investment in our technical ecosystem.”

Rao started out as a graphic designer and 3D artist. From there he worked his way through documentary and feature editing, scanning, lab work, visual effects and color grading. That time spent across departments taught him where crews run into trouble during the shoot and in post, and at Annapurna he has used it to find weak spots in workflows and build infrastructure around what productions actually need.

“We have always believed that understanding the filmmaker’s requirements is just as important as understanding the technology itself,” he says.

Annapurna has put money into virtual production and one of India’s largest motion-capture stages. Interest is growing, but uptake has lagged expectations. During COVID, the studio saw virtual production as a way for filmmakers to stay inside controlled environments and cut down on location work and travel. The format, though, demands heavy preparation: digital sets have to be built, and camera moves and lighting mapped out, before anyone shoots a frame.

“In our industry, approvals and shooting schedules often get finalized very late,” Rao says. “Even when filmmakers are interested, there may not be enough time to prepare everything properly. Under these circumstances, conventional shooting becomes the easier option.”

Motion capture is gaining ground on complex action scenes, digital characters and performance-driven VFX. Rao expects both technologies to become standard tools once more productions have hands-on experience and learn where they genuinely save time.

On infrastructure, he maintains that Indian facilities hold their own against established international hubs and can access most of the same advanced technology. Several major Indian productions now finish large chunks of their post work at home. Where India trails is research and development. Mature markets, he says, benefit from close collaboration among studios, technology companies, research institutions and creatives, while India needs to spend more on homegrown technology and on testing new workflows.

“We strongly believe infrastructure alone cannot deliver quality,” Rao says. “The knowledge, experience and creativity of the professionals behind it make the real difference.”

Annapurna has also set up Dolby Vision and Dolby Atmos mastering facilities. Rao finds big-budget productions increasingly sold on premium formats, while mid-budget filmmakers are still weighing whether they pay off commercially. With streaming and high-end TVs giving audiences quality viewing at home, he sees premium theatrical formats as a reason to get people back into cinemas.

“However, there needs to be better alignment between mastering capabilities and theatrical exhibition infrastructure,” Rao says. As more premium-format screens open across India, he expects producers to commit to these formats earlier in their release plans.

AI is already proving useful in concept development, storyboarding, previsualization, restoration and repetitive VFX tasks, letting teams test ideas faster and automate slow work.

“However, storytelling, performances, emotional expression and final artistic decisions must remain under human creative control,” he says.

He sees AI working alongside existing tools rather than replacing them. Complex digital characters driven by performance may still need precise motion capture, with AI handling supporting jobs.

Whether any of this lowers costs depends on the project. Virtual production can trim location, travel and scheduling expenses, but it requires investment in infrastructure, digital assets and prep, and without proper planning it can end up costing more. AI is getting easier for smaller filmmakers to access, yet consistent professional results still call for skilled teams, computing power and quality control.

“We believe smaller productions can benefit from shared facilities and specialized technology services rather than investing in expensive infrastructure independently,” Rao says.

Having overseen technical pipelines for more than 500 feature-film deliveries, Rao traces most post-production trouble back to poor planning. The most frequent error is bringing post teams in too late, when VFX, sound, grading, data management and delivery specs should ideally be on the table in pre-production. Late creative changes are the other big problem. Changes come with the territory, he acknowledges, but major revisions near delivery put artists and technicians under enormous strain.

“Teams may need to work extended hours, repeat completed tasks and rush important processes to meet deadlines,” Rao says. “This creates unnecessary stress, increases costs and can sometimes compromise the quality of the final product.”

For India to land complete high-end international productions, Rao wants a friendlier business climate, consistent government policy, competitive incentives and simpler approvals, along with deeper partnerships with global studios and more spending on research, training and integrated facilities.

“The opportunity is to position India not merely as a cost-effective outsourcing destination, but as a reliable creative and technical partner capable of handling complete productions to international standards,” he says.

Training is part of that equation. Rao wants studios to offer structured internships and mentorship, educational institutions to rework curricula alongside working professionals, and government to back specialized training centers and research.

As an SMPTE Fellow, he plans to take part in technical discussions where India’s experience can help. An industry that works across many languages and production scales, under demanding workflows, has lessons to offer in digital cinema, post-production and data management, he says. He frames the fellowship as a two-way exchange, bringing ideas from global engineers and researchers back home in return.

Looking five years out, Rao expects AI, real-time rendering, virtual production and motion capture to reshape filmmaking as they increasingly converge. Annapurna will keep upgrading its infrastructure, exploring new workflows and training teams, judging each new technology on whether it solves real production problems rather than on novelty.

“Ultimately, we believe technology investments should be driven by the value they bring to filmmaking, not simply by technological advancement itself,” Rao says.

"No One Gets Left Behind" - CDPR On The Witcher 4 Releasing On All Platforms At The Same Time

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

"Switch 2 is clearly getting left behind"

This week, CD Projekt Red and Sony announced an exclusive marketing deal for Ciri's upcoming title, The Witcher 4. It's raised some concerns online about the fourth outing potentially being a "timed-exclusive" for the PS5, but thankfully, this won't be the case.

CD Projekt Red's co-CEO Michal Nowakowski confirmed this to a fan via social media, but his response about how "no one gets left behind" (in reference to previously announced versions of the game) appears to have upset some Nintendo fans, as there's currently no confirmation of a Switch 2 version.

"All the platforms release at the same time - PS5, XB, and PC. No one gets left behind."

The Witcher 4 was unveiled for PS5, Xbox and PC in 2024, and there's been no mention of a Nintendo release since then. While this doesn't completely rule out a Switch 2 port in the future, Nintendo fans have noted how the "Switch 2 is clearly getting left behind" in this case.

Of course, we'll remain hopeful given CDPR's strong support of Nintendo's platforms in the past and more recently. The fourth game, powered by Unreal Engine 5, also isn't expected until 2028, so anything could happen between now and then.

The Polish studio previously worked its magic (with the help of Saber Interactive) during the Switch generation, with an "impossible" port of Witcher 3. Most recently, Switch 2 players got Witcher 3 Remastered, and this is set to be followed by the new expansion 'Songs of the Past' in 2027. CDPR also did a "preem" job on the Switch 2 version of Cyberpunk 2077.

Could Bitwise’s $667M XRP ETF inflows come to the rescue as bear trap takes shape?

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Bitwise has released its annual XRP ETF report.

According to the report, the ETF has seen net cash flows of $667 million since it began trading on the NYSE in November 2025. Meanwhile, assets under management were in excess of $376 million while total trading crossed $3.5 billion, averaging around $14 million a day.

In essence, despite XRP’s lackluster performance, interest in the ETF continues to receive institutional investment, implying a high level of investor demand.

In a post on X, Bitwise highlighted XRP’s decade-long presence in the crypto market. It also noted how the asset has remained relevant through multiple market cycles and continues to shape the industry’s future.

XRP has been part of crypto’s story for more than a decade, through every cycle, and it’s still helping shape what comes next. We’re grateful to everyone who’s been part of the journey so far. Here’s to year two!

The timing is particularly interesting here.

Bitwise’s report comes as XRP’s technical and on-chain metrics continue to deteriorate. Meanwhile, prediction markets are also increasingly pricing in a year-end target below $0.80. Against this backdrop, the key question is whether Bitwise’s report can strengthen institutional interest and turn the current bearish setup into a bear trap.

The impact of Bitwise’s one-year XRP ETF performance could already be triggering FOMO.

Notably, another Wall Street firm has disclosed exposure to an XRP ETF. Atlanta-based Arkadios Wealth Advisors, which looks after $8.79 billion in client assets, stated this in its most recent SEC document.

Furthermore, there is some institutional interest in Turkiye too. One of the nation’s biggest private banks, Yapi Kredi, is leveraging Ripple Custody to provide digital asset security solutions.

The key takeaway? XRP’s derivatives continue to be significantly net short, with the long/short ratio showing a pronounced sell-side sentiment. At the time of writing, XRP seemed set to close its 10th consecutive weekly down candle, with broader market FUD and prediction markets pricing in a year-end target below $0.80 to reinforce the cautious outlook.

Interestingly, however, the XRP/BTC ratio has risen by almost 5% following three successive weeks of upside.

In essence, broader market weakness could create room for more capital to flow into XRP.

This makes it an important setup to watch over the coming months. The logic is simple —  Entering Q4, market participants are already factoring in further downside potential. In this context, Bitwise’s recent report, growing ETF exposure, and Ripple’s expansion in Turkiye could encourage further capital rotation into XRP.

If this trend continues, XRP’s ongoing consolidation around $0.90 could turn out to be an accumulation phase before a potential bear trap.

Bitwise’s XRP ETF has attracted $667 million in net inflows, while growing institutional exposure and Ripple’s expansion in Turkiye point to sustained demand.

Despite bearish derivatives and weakening market sentiment, rising XRP/BTC strength could signal a potential accumulation phase.
