How Jump-Roping Toy Soldiers Accidentally Took Over Your FYP

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Holidave, the stalwart leader of SeaWorld’s jump-roping toy soldiers troupe, is known for his ability to jump rope while sitting down, “a skill only shared by 0.1 percent of Earth’s population.”

He’s the most “laid-back” of all the toy soldiers, allowing fellow toy soldier Jackary Jingleson to take center stage. And “experts” and “recent polling data,” “fans believe it is Holidave who holds the most whimsy” out of all the SeaWorld toy soldiers.

This is all according to AI slop videos posted by @wejumprope_memes, a fan page devoted to the group of masked professional jump ropers, who perform an annual holiday show at SeaWorld Orlando. Per toy soldier lore, in addition to Jackary Jingleson there’s also Sugarsnookie, Soulja Toy, Big Chestnut, and Frosty McSleigh. And all of the edits are the same: an AI-generated narrator recites stats about the toy soldiers, such as their net worth and their vertical leap, as if they were elite athletes and not niche theme park performers.

For the past two weeks, fan page videos featuring the jump-roping toy soldiers have been going viral, with @wejumprope_memes’ most popular video garnering 1.2 million views. In the comments sections, fans debate the merits and deficits of each toy soldier, declaring their allegiance to their favorite as if they were a ’90s boy band: “Jackery Jingleson is having a banner year! His speed is incomparable, his style undeniable, and his technique is utterly flawless,” reads one comment. Another disputes this, writing “Soulja Toy the underrated gem.”

Mostly, people seem confused as to why these videos keep showing up in their algorithm and why they keep watching them. “I have a PhD and lecture at a university. And yet here I am,” one commenter writes on a video about whether Major Bounce, a new toy soldier, will show up for the 2026 season. (In a November 20 Reel announcing “major news for the jump-roping toy soldier community,” he finally did.)

As it turns out, there’s a simple explanation for the virality. @wejumprope_memes is run by Devin Meek, an Orlando-based professional entertainer who works for WeFlip, a production company that produces acrobatics shows. But he’s now perhaps best known as his alter ego—Holidave.

What started just a few weeks ago as him making memes to make his coworkers laugh has metamorphosed into an internet sensation, with the toy soldiers amassing a genuine, and ardent, fandom. “I really just made these videos for my friends in the jump-roping community,” Meek tells WIRED. “But I guess they resonated with more people than I thought.”

The videos are piggybacking on a larger—and increasingly absurd—trend of seasonal theme park employees going viral. In September, as WIRED previously reported, SeaWorld Orlando enjoyed a bump in ticket sales thanks to Just One More Chapter, a BookTok-themed Halloween scare zone at SeaWorld Orlando that featured swaggering bad boys from the romantasy genre flirting with guests. And last month, women on TikTok swooned over Archer and Fryboy, two performers at Hersheypark Halloween known for sliding around in kneepads wearing tactical streetwear and clown makeup, a practice known as “sliding.”

Meek, who has been jumping rope for the past 20 years, was inspired to create the videos featuring Sugarsnookie, Jackary, and the like after seeing the success of Archer and Fryboy, who have a combined more than 3.1 million followers on TikTok. “It was such an interesting phenomenon, because I’m somebody who is in a very similar position where I work a few months out of the year as a holiday-themed performer,” he says. “So I got a kick out of it.”

Meek already had an account, @wejumprope_memes, where he posted jump-roping fails and compilations. He posted one video on November 13 featuring old footage from SeaWorld Orlando, where he’s been performing with the other toy soldiers for the past six years. (The group got started during the height of the Covid pandemic—hence, why they wear masks during their shows.) “Fryboy who? Move over, Archer,” it begins, before introducing the lore of Jackery Jingleson, the most “light-hearted, nonchalant” of the toy soldiers.

To Meek’s surprise, the video received almost a million views and 588 comments, most of which were not from fellow members of the jumping community but from total strangers. So Meek posted more toy soldier lore on his meme page, introducing Big Chestnut (“a powerful jumper”), Soulja Toy (the developer of the “Christmas Ice Storm 261”), Sugarsnookie (“the girl’s girl of the group,” who is dating Big Chestnut) and Frosty McSleigh (“he has the fastest salute time of all the toy soldiers.”)

Though the lore is fictional, it’s also based on the jumpers’ actual skills and personalities: the jumpers who play Big Chestnut and Sugarsnookie are actually in a long-term relationship, and Jackery Jingleson, perhaps the most popular of the group, once held the world record for the fastest jump roper in the United States. “Honestly, that makes [the popularity of the memes] even funnier to us,” he says.

Though some fans have speculated in the comments that the toy soldier memes are a marketing campaign for SeaWorld Orlando, Meek denies that’s the case: He doesn’t tag SeaWorld in the comments, nor has he been in contact with the theme park about his page. (SeaWorld Orlando did not respond to WIRED’s request for comment.)

“This is something that is so targeted to people who are internet-brained and have this ironic sense of humor,” he says. “And that’s exactly the type of person who’ll tune out immediately if they feel like they’re being sold something on the internet.”

Others have even speculated that there is an actual feud brewing between the SeaWorld Orlando “jumpers” and the Southern California–based “sliders,” though Meek says the rivalry is playful, and he has even reached out to collab with them. (The sliders may view it differently: So far, he says, they “don’t seem super interested” in joining forces.)

What has surprised Meek the most, however, is that very few commenters suspect that the toy soldiers themselves are behind the memes. In fact, he’s been shocked by how many organic meme fan pages have popped up within the past week, with people drawing their own fan art of Holidave saluting or Jackery Jingleson jumping. WeFlip has also been deluged with business inquiries: “People are like, how can we get Jackery Jingleson out here?”

Essentially, what started as an inside joke within an extremely niche community has evolved into an inside joke for the internet at large. “I think people like the feeling of being in on a joke and being a part of it and building it up,” he says.

So what’s next for the SeaWorld Orlando jump-roping toy soldiers? The rotating cast typically performs at the park on the weekends, ramping up appearances as it gets closer to the holiday season. Meek also shares that the toy soldiers will “likely cross state lines” in the coming months, including a stint at the Christmas market in Cullman, Alabama. Frosty McSleigh, renowned for his 0.08-second salute, plus a slew of new, yet-to-be-introduced toy soldiers will also make an appearance.

Though it may come as a disappointment to some that the roots of the toy soldier phenomenon were inorganic, Meek isn’t concerned. “The people are real. The athleticism is real. The lore is real,” he says. “For all of these people, jump rope is their life, and we’re super-dedicated to teaching it and sharing it. And I think people can feel that.”

Kimchi Bolognese

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Food  |  Read Source →

I’ve always loved Italian American food. Maybe because Italian and Korean cooking have traits in common: Both are homey and resourceful, and grandmothers are revered for their signature dishes made without formal recipes. This recipe was one of the first Korean Italian fusion recipes I developed, and it makes use of my two favorite secret weapons: kimchi and fish sauce. The result is the Bolognese you know and love, but with a deep, tangy twist. Fish sauce in Italian cooking is not a new idea, actually—Romans have been using salted anchovies and garum, an ancient kind of fish sauce, for centuries. For an extra-special finish, I love garnishing this with baek (white) kimchi for acidity. But it’s optional, and regular kimchi (or no kimchi) works just fine, too.

Start the Bolognese sauce: Heat 2 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil in a large Dutch oven or pot over medium-high heat. Stir in 1 medium-yellow onion, finely diced (about 1½ cups), 2 carrots, peeled and finely diced, and 3 stalks celery, finely diced, and cook, stirring occasionally, until the onions have softened and the edges start to brown, about 4 minutes. Stir in 4 cloves garlic, minced, and a pinch of kosher salt and cook for 30 seconds, then stir in 1 cup chopped kimchi and 1 Tbsp. tomato paste and cook until the kimchi pieces start to soften, about 3 minutes. Pour in ½ cup red wine, increase the heat, and simmer, stirring with a wooden spoon to dislodge any bits that have stuck to the bottom, until the wine has nearly disappeared, about 2 minutes.

Add the meat: Stir in 1 lb. ground beef, season with generous pinches of salt (at least ½ tsp.), and cook, breaking up the beef into small pieces with a wooden spoon, until cooked through, 6 to 8 minutes.

Add the tomatoes: Pour one 28-oz. can whole San Marzano tomatoes in a bowl. Using clean hands, squish the tomatoes into small pieces. Pour the tomatoes into the pot and add 1 cup beef broth or water, ½ tsp. dried Italian seasoning or dried oregano, a pinch of salt, and a few grinds of freshly ground black pepper. Bring to a simmer and lower the heat to medium-low. Gently cook, stirring occasionally, until the sauce is just thick enough to stand up on a spoon, about 20 minutes. Stir in 2 Tbsp. fish sauce and taste, then add more salt or fish sauce if needed.

Cook the noodles and serve: In a pot of boiling salted water, cook 1 lb. spaghetti according to package instructions, stirring occasionally to prevent sticking, until al dente. Drain the noodles in a colander, then give the colander a shake to get rid of extra water. Divide the noodles among serving plates and spoon the Bolognese on top. Garnish with 1 cup chopped kimchi (white or regular; if using) and 2 Tbsp. chopped flat-leaf parsley and serve.

Do Ahead: Leftover Bolognese sauce (without the noodles) keeps in an airtight container in the refrigerator for up to 5 days, or in the freezer for up to 2 months.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/catching-new-ceo-unitedhealthcares-surest" hreflang="en">Catching up with the new CEO of UnitedHealthcare's Surest</a>

📅 2026-11-25  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Catching up with the new CEO of UnitedHealthcare's Surest" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Joe Clark is stepping into the CEO chair at UnitedHealthcare's Surest unit with an eye on affordability.

Clark was appointed to lead the division earlier this month, after serving as the chief financial officer for the insurer's employer and individual segment. In an interview with Fierce Healthcare, he said that the new role comes at "an exciting time" to be a part of a plan like Surest.

Under the model, Surest eliminates deductibles for a copayment-only approach, where members' costs are tied to the quality and value for their provider. Members connect to the Surest app or website to compare the cost between different providers.

"Affordability is a challenge every year, but especially right now, for employers," Clark said. "We've seen demand for Surest and other sort of alternative health plans just skyrocket over the last year. And being part of being able to deliver that and offer solutions to employers is incredibly exciting."

UnitedHealthcare first launched the unit in 2016 as Bind before rebranding as Surest in 2022 with an eye on growth. In a 2023 report, the company said that Surest members had 6% fewer emergency department visits and 13% fewer inpatient admissions compared to people enrolled in traditional commercial plans at the same company.

The plan also saw a 20% increase in visits to a physician and 9% increase in preventative physical exams compared to the members in traditional plans.

In a 2024 analysis, conducted by Aon, the company found that Surest enrollees generated $400 or more in cost claims savings per year. That study also identified improvements around spending and utilization across all age groups and accounting for several conditions.

Clark said that in a traditional health plan, only about 15% of people hit their deductible for the year, meaning they're ultimately not using their insurance during that time frame. Surest removes that problem by eliminating the deductible and puts the power in the consumers' hands to make choices for their own care.

He added that the Net Promoter Score for Surest is higher than its other commercial plan offerings. Consumers report satisfaction with the digital experience and the cost certainty that the plan presents, Clark said.

"It's very much like the Amazon shopping experience, but for healthcare, which hasn't happened before," Clark said.

Surest also aims to personalize the member experience by leaning on AI and other digital tools to present data on providers and costs that may be of use to the user, while leaving the ultimate decision in their hands.

"The consumer is in charge with their doctor in making those decisions," Clark said. "But we want to make sure that we are giving you, similar to what you would see with any shopping app, a personalized recommendation of what is a good fit."

Clark said that starting his career on the actuarial side of healthcare made plain how much of a challenge affordability can be, and that drew him to pursue his current path. Prior to UnitedHealthcare, he served as an associate partner at McKinsey and as an actuary at Cigna.

Through Surest, he believes he can help lead the charge toward a more modern healthcare system, he said.

As for the future of Surest, the first piece is continuing to make sure the team delivers on a personalized and simple-to-understand experience for consumers. The next level is building on that consumer-focused experience to add a provider layer, and work with them on the quality and value scores that are surfaced to members.

For example, a provider may consistently recommend a patient seek an MRI scan at a hospital rather than at a lower-cost lab. And Surest is gaining the "critical mass" of members where providers actively want to have these conversations, Clark said.

"I see that as sort of the next evolution beyond working with consumers and members: moving to providers as well," he said.

Babysitting Co-op – Trade Hours With Other Families Instead of Paying a Sitter

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. A babysitting co-op lets you trade hours of watching other families’ kids for hours of free sitting when you need it, for date nights or appointments. Join one near you or start one with five to 15 families you trust, track the hours in points, and cap how many points any family can owe.

Babysitters on Care.com post rates averaging about $20 an hour nationwide, so a four-hour date night costs about $80 in babysitting alone. With no family nearby, you pay that rate for every appointment and night out.

A co-op replaces that $80 with points. You earn one point for every hour you watch a child, and you spend one point for every hour someone watches one of yours. Points count across the whole group, so you can babysit for one family and spend the points with another.

Say you have two kids and watch one child from each of two other families for three hours on a Saturday afternoon. That earns six points, enough for a three-hour date night the next weekend.

To start one, invite five to 15 families who know each other, such as parents from your child’s preschool or your block. Then put the rules in writing before anyone babysits:

Book sitters and track points in a shared calendar and spreadsheet, or pay $15 a year per family for SittingAround. It sends each request for a sitter to the whole group, tracks every family’s points, and lists co-ops near you that you can ask to join.

If you can’t find five families, trade babysitting with one other family on alternate Saturday nights, or book one paid sitter for several families’ kids at one house and split the bill.

Search for a co-op near you, or start your own, on SittingAround.

Emergency repairs at Etruscan tombs in Italy uncovered stone thrones and a jar that looters had missed

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Archaeologists in Cerveteri, Italy, uncovered Etruscan tombs with stone thrones, red-painted walls and a decorated jar overlooked by looters.

Emergency work at the Monte Abatone necropolis in Cerveteri, the Etruscan city of Caere, has turned up stone thrones, walls painted red, and a nearly intact decorated jar.

The work was carried out by Italy’s heritage authority for the province of Viterbo and southern Etruria, the Soprintendenza Archeologia, Belle Arti e Paesaggio. Some of the tombs had collapsed or become unstable.

Clandestine diggers had entered these tombs before the archaeologists arrived. They left the jar behind.

The collapses and instability meant the tombs had to be investigated, documented and secured. The team cleared the entrances and, as a precaution, removed the fill that had built up inside.

The chambers were dug into tuff, a soft volcanic rock. Excavators have also found places on the hill where blocks of tuff were quarried in several stages.

During a 2019 excavation, a team including the University of Bonn documented a Monte Abatone tomb whose ceiling had partly fallen in along a crack.

The researchers linked such cracks to movements of the tuff layer over a less stable layer of clay. The Soprintendenza has not said what caused the latest collapses, or when they occurred.

One of the chambers still keeps much of its original vaulted ceiling.

In another Etruscan chamber tomb, the funerary furniture was carved directly out of the tuff, including thrones.

A third chamber has extensive red painted decoration on its walls.

Similar furnishings are known from earlier work at Monte Abatone. In 2019, years before the emergency repairs, excavators recorded kline-type death beds, shaped like couches, and narrower benches lining the walls of another chamber.

Cerveteri’s larger Banditaccia necropolis and the Monterozzi necropolis at Tarquinia together form a UNESCO World Heritage property. At Banditaccia, thousands of tombs are laid out in a city-like plan, with streets and small squares.

The most complete find is an olla stamnoide, a jar shaped like a stamnos. It is almost intact. Its surface carries incised figures of animals, with some details picked out in red paint.

The jar will be cleaned and restored, then put on public display. No date or venue for the display has been announced.

The earlier clandestine digging compromised the original context of the burials. Some materials survived it and were recovered.

Looters have missed objects at Monte Abatone before. In the 2019 excavation, Tomb 642 showed signs of their digging, yet it still held a large pithos, a bucchero drinking set, Ionian bowls and Corinthian ointment jars.

Monte Abatone lies immediately south of the ancient city. It flourished in the seventh and sixth centuries BCE, according to a 2022 report on Monte Abatone.

In the 1950s and 1960s, surveys and excavations explored 641 tombs there. That work was carried out on behalf of the heritage authority in response to heavy looting.

Burial types at the site include simple trench graves, chamber tombs and tombs under burial mounds, among them the Campana Tumulus.

That great mound dates to the middle of the Orientalizing period and had not been surveyed since the first half of the 19th century.

Older finds show that burials there continued from the late eighth century BCE into the Hellenistic period and the early Roman Empire.

Researchers also examined the area west and north of the Campana Tumulus. It held tombs of the sixth to third centuries BCE, excavated in the past and partly backfilled, and funerary terraces facing the city.

A research project on Monte Abatone began in the early 1990s. After a new project was set up in 2017, a group of Italian and German universities ran excavation campaigns at the site from 2018 to 2021.

Ground-penetrating radar was used across an area of about 7.4 acres (3 hectares) east of the Campana Tumulus. An earlier geophysical test had revealed a densely packed cluster of tombs.

A 2021 trial excavation opened about 13,500 square feet (1,250 square meters) south of the great tumulus. It exposed tombs with small mounds, up to about 36 feet (11 meters) across, set in two roughly parallel rows and all facing northwest.

Excavations on the south side also uncovered what the team describes as a “possible ‘family’ cluster of tombs,” used from the early Orientalizing period into the Archaic period.

“This suggests the existence of a planning by some form of ‘urban’ authority,” the authors of the 2022 report wrote about the rows of mounds.

Laser scanners recorded the site in fine detail, producing a combined model of about 4.3 billion points, roughly 1.2 billion of them for the Campana Tumulus alone. A drone survey added a photogrammetric model of the plateau.

The information was obtained from the Soprintendenza for the province of Viterbo and southern Etruria.

Compare models on held-out observations, not training fit alone

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A fourth-degree polynomial can pass through five training observations almost perfectly. That does not automatically make it a better predictor than a quadratic. This Python example compares three model degrees on a fixed set of separate observations.

I ran this script in PythonIDE on an English iPhone simulator. All observations are fictional.

The input split is explicit and disjoint. polyfit estimates coefficients on training observations only, while polyval evaluates the same coefficients on both sets. Report training and held-out RMSE separately, then plot all models over the observed range. The example never claims the highest-degree model must be worst on every dataset.

Once held-out results are used to choose a degree, that split is a validation set. It is no longer an untouched final test. A further independent dataset is needed for a final performance claim. Nine fictional observations do not support broad scientific conclusions, and the polynomial units and extrapolation behavior need care.

Only the .py script was tested in PythonIDE. If you adapt it to a notebook, separate setup, fitting, and display into cells, then restart and run all before comparing screenshots. Change only one modeling decision at a time and record it; stale notebook variables can otherwise make the displayed curve disagree with the code.

Change one training response by two units and compare all three curves and both error columns. Then explain why repeatedly choosing models on the held-out set consumes its independence.

I build PythonIDE, a Python coding app for iPhone and iPad. AI assisted with drafting and translation; the script, numerical output and graph were checked in PythonIDE on an iPhone simulator.

Observed run: degree 1 training/held-out RMSE 6.780/5.005; degree 2 0.192/0.154; degree 4 0.000/0.194. Degree 2 wins this small validation comparison, despite degree 4 fitting the training data more closely.

How to Trace Jetpack Compose 1.13 Recompositions and Automate Builds With Android CLI 1.1

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 25 • Originally published at mangodeveloper.com

Three core Android developer releases converged between November 13 and November 19, 2026: Android CLI 1.1.16919734, SDK Platform-Tools 37.0.3, and the stable Jetpack Compose November '26 release (version 1.13, BOM 2026.11.01). Together, they move several workflows that previously required keeping a full Android Studio window open, including Jetpack Compose recomposition state tracing, lightweight Perfetto object-graph heap dumps, @Preview PNG rendering, and Firebase Crashlytics field diagnostics, directly into scriptable terminal commands.

If you already connected an external coding agent to your IDE using our guide on how to connect Claude, Codex or Antigravity to Android Studio Rabbit 2, configuring the November 2026 command-line toolchain is the natural next step. Giving your local terminal or CI runner direct access to android profiler, android preview, android input, and android analyze lets you verify UI changes, measure R8 DEX footprints, and pinpoint unnecessary Compose state writes before opening a pull request.

We have not benchmarked these tools on our own physical device rack; every command flag, Gradle dependency coordinate, environment variable, and API signature below comes directly from Google's official Android CLI, SDK Platform-Tools, and Jetpack Compose 1.13 documentation.

Before editing build scripts, it helps to see how the three November 2026 updates fit together. Android CLI 1.1.16919734 acts as the high-level terminal orchestrator for building, deploying, profiling, and interacting with apps. Underneath it, SDK Platform-Tools 37.0.3 overhauls adb transport reliability on Linux, macOS (including Apple Silicon M6 running macOS 27), and Windows while retiring legacy incremental flags. Inside the app binary itself, Jetpack Compose 1.13 (androidx.compose:compose-bom:2026.11.01) exposes the runtime hooks and performance APIs that the CLI profiler reads.

The table below maps the key November 2026 capabilities across their required tool versions and minimum target constraints.

Understanding those version boundaries prevents the most common upgrade failures, such as passing --enable=compose.recomposition against an older Compose 1.12 runtime or watching CI scripts break because adb install --fastdeploy was deprecated in Platform-Tools 37.0.3.

You can install or upgrade Android CLI through standard system package managers (apt-get, winget, or homebrew) or manage your SDK packages directly from the terminal. Once the binary is on your PATH, update your platform tools to revision 37.0.3 and enable shell autocompletion so subcommands expand cleanly in Bash or Zsh.

android completion zsh > "${fpath[1]}/_android"

Because Android CLI 1.0.16406183 and newer take an inter-process lock during android sdk operations and unpack native downloader libraries lazily only when network calls occur, startup is roughly twice as fast as mid-2026 builds and safe to invoke across concurrent CI jobs.

SDK Platform-Tools 37.0.3 changes how adb talks to physical USB buses on Linux and macOS. Earlier releases toggled between libusb and kernel netlink hotplug events, which occasionally dropped devices during in-place USB resets or concurrent adb start-server race conditions. Revision 37.0.3 introduces libadbusb as the default USB backend on both Linux and macOS (matching the Windows rollout from 37.0.1).

On Linux, libadbusb polls the USB bus every 1000ms by default rather than relying exclusively on netlink hotplug events. You can tune that polling interval or fall back to the legacy USB backend using environment variables before starting the server:

adb kill-server ADB_USB_LINUX_POLL_INTERVAL_MS=500 adb start-server

adb kill-server ADB_USB_LEGACY=1 adb start-server

On macOS, revision 37.0.3 also patches two severe crashes: an _Unwind_Backtrace abort on Apple Silicon M6 machines running macOS 27 triggered by non-ADB mDNS records, and a KERN_INVALID_NAME Mach port exception when attaching a USB phone on systems with Apple Mobile File Integrity (AMFI) disabled.

If you prefer testing over Wi-Fi without copying six-digit pairing codes by hand, 37.0.3 adds terminal QR code generation directly inside adb:

Scan the terminal QR code from Settings > Developer options > Wireless debugging > Pair device with QR code on your test phone, and adb completes the TLS handshake automatically.

Comparison diagram of Android CLI Delta Install Engine and ADB 37.0.3 libadbusb architecture

Platform-Tools 37.0.3 officially deprecates adb install --fastdeploy in favor of android install and android run. While --deprecated-fastdeploy remains as a temporary bridge flag in adb, Google's active incremental deployment work now lives inside Android CLI's delta install engine.

In Android CLI 1.1.16919734, delta installs gained repositioned-file reuse alongside zero-copy kernel system calls. Previously, if a small code edit shifted the byte offset of unchanged asset or DEX entries inside the packaged APK, the installer had to resend those shifted blocks over USB or Wi-Fi. Now, android install and android run detect unchanged files even when their position inside the APK changes, keeping incremental pushes fast across large multi-module builds:

android install app/build/outputs/apk/debug/app-debug.apk

android install --use-delta-install-instructions=COPY,DATA app/build/outputs/apk/debug/app-debug.apk

Note that positional build references such as :app automatically default to the debug build variant in Android CLI 1.1, saving repetitive flag typing during local iteration.

To use the November 2026 recomposition profiler and static analysis checks, upgrade your application to the Jetpack Compose November '26 stable release. Version 1.13 across core Compose artifacts requires Kotlin 2.2 or newer and compileSdk 37.1.

Open your module's build.gradle.kts file and update compileSdk, the Compose BOM, and the runtime-tracing dependency required by android profiler systemtrace:

gradle android { namespace = "com.example.telemetryapp" compileSdk = 37 compileSdkExtension = 1 // or compileSdkPreview / compileSdk 37.1 syntax per AGP setup

gradle defaultConfig { minSdk = 26 targetSdk = 37 }

gradle testOptions { unitTests.isIncludeAndroidResources = true // Optional: enable Compose 1.13 failure artifacts globally across instrumentation tests execution = "ANDROIDX_TEST_ORCHESTRATOR" animationsDisabled = true } }

gradle dependencies { val composeBom = platform("androidx.compose:compose-bom:2026.11.01") implementation(composeBom) androidTestImplementation(composeBom)

gradle implementation("androidx.compose.foundation:foundation") implementation("androidx.compose.material3:material3:1.5.0") implementation("androidx.compose.ui:ui") implementation("androidx.compose.ui:ui-tooling-preview") debugImplementation("androidx.compose.ui:ui-tooling")

gradle // Required for Android CLI 1.1.16919734 Compose composition and recomposition tracing implementation("androidx.compose.runtime:runtime-tracing:1.13.0") }

Also verify that your AndroidManifest.xml includes profileable android:shell="true" inside the application manifest tag so Perfetto system traces and heap dumps can attach to release-like builds without full debuggable overhead.

Tracking down why a screen stutters during scrolling used to mean guessing which MutableState triggered a cascade of recompositions. By pairing androidx.compose.runtime:runtime-tracing:1.13.0 with Android CLI 1.1.16919734, you can capture every composable execution slice alongside the exact state reads, state writes, and stack traces that caused each invalidation.

Three-step profiling pipeline from Compose BOM 2026.11.01 to android profiler and Perfetto Trace ZIP

Android CLI splits Compose system tracing into two granularity levels so you can balance diagnostic detail against recording overhead:

Run the lifecycle commands below from your project root while a device or headless emulator is connected:

android profiler systemtrace prepare --package com.example.telemetryapp

android profiler systemtrace start \ --package com.example.telemetryapp \ --enable=compose.recomposition

android profiler systemtrace stop --output traces/checkout-recomposition-trace.zip

android profiler session list android profiler session status

Because recording state string representations and stack traces on every write adds measurable CPU overhead, always disable compose.recomposition when benchmarking raw frame timing, and switch back to compose.composition or standard system traces once you have isolated the offending state mutation. The resulting .zip archive opens directly inside the Android Studio Profiler or Perfetto UI.

Traditional .hprof JVM heap dumps capture every primitive array and string value in memory, resulting in multi-hundred-megabyte files that take ages to pull over wireless debugging. Android CLI 1.1.16919734 adds --type=perfetto to android profiler heapdump capture for devices running Android 12 (API level 31) or higher:

android profiler heapdump capture \ --package com.example.telemetryapp \ --type=perfetto \ --output traces/leak-graph.perfetto-trace

android profiler heapdump capture \ --package com.example.telemetryapp \ --output traces/full-memory.hprof

Because a Perfetto heap dump records object instances and the reference edges between them while skipping raw field payloads, it transfers in a fraction of the time and immediately answers the core memory-leak question: which long-lived singleton or callback is still holding a reference to a destroyed screen or composable state holder.

Once your trace highlights an expensive composable or a janky exit transition, Jetpack Compose 1.13 provides several new primitives designed to cut invalidation overhead and eliminate mid-animation state flicker. You can also audit your repository automatically by installing the official Compose performance static analysis skill through Android CLI:

android skills find compose android skills add jetpack-compose-performance jetpack-compose-adaptive android skills list

The performance skill audits your code across seven categories: state and stability, lazy layouts, general layouts, graphics, animation, effects and threading, and Modifier.Node migrations. Below are the four high-impact code patterns introduced in Compose 1.13 that resolve the most frequent findings.

Developers frequently wrap scroll offsets or animation progress checks inside derivedStateOf to prevent a composable from recomposing on every pixel scrolled. However, derivedStateOf allocates and maintains an internal cache object. When the calculation itself is trivial, such as comparing a scroll index against a threshold, maintaining that cache costs more overhead than the boolean comparison itself.

Compose 1.13 introduces computedStateOf, which skips result caching while still invalidating the reading composition only when the computed output changes:

kotlin import androidx.compose.foundation.lazy.LazyListState import androidx.compose.runtime.Composable import androidx.compose.runtime.computedStateOf import androidx.compose.runtime.getValue import androidx.compose.runtime.remember

kotlin @Composable fun FeedScreenWithScrollTopButton(listState: LazyListState) { // computedStateOf recalculates without caching overhead and only invalidates // FeedScreenWithScrollTopButton when the boolean result flips between true and false. val showScrollToTop by remember { computedStateOf { listState.firstVisibleItemIndex > 4 } }

jsx if (showScrollToTop) { ScrollToTopFab() } }

Reserve derivedStateOf for genuinely expensive transformations (such as sorting or filtering a large in-memory list), and default to computedStateOf for threshold checks, boolean conditions, and fast arithmetic over high-frequency states.

With standard AnimatedVisibility, Compose keeps composing the outgoing content throughout the entire exit animation. When a user dismisses a detail sheet by setting selectedItem = null, the exiting composable suddenly sees null mid-animation, forcing you either to cache a stale copy of the state manually or watch the card vanish into a blank frame before the fade-out finishes.

Compose 1.13 adds CapturedAnimatedVisibility, which snapshots the final rendered frame the moment visible turns false and animates that snapshot out while releasing the underlying state immediately:

kotlin import androidx.compose.animation.CapturedAnimatedVisibility import androidx.compose.animation.core.tween import androidx.compose.animation.expandVertically import androidx.compose.animation.fadeIn import androidx.compose.animation.fadeOut import androidx.compose.animation.shrinkVertically import androidx.compose.runtime.Composable

kotlin @Composable fun OrderDetailsSheet(activeOrder: OrderSummary?) { // When activeOrder becomes null, CapturedAnimatedVisibility immediately unmounts // OrderCard and animates a frozen snapshot of its last frame, running 7-8% faster on exit. CapturedAnimatedVisibility( visible = activeOrder != null, enter = fadeIn() + expandVertically(), exit = fadeOut(tween(400)) + shrinkVertically(tween(400)), ) { if (activeOrder != null) { OrderCard(order = activeOrder) } } }

According to Google's Compose 1.13 release benchmarks, freeing the outgoing composition immediately reduces exit frame times by 7 to 8 percent compared to AnimatedVisibility. Keep AnimatedVisibility only when the exiting UI must stay interactive during the transition or when children rely on Modifier.animateEnterExit().

In Compose 1.13, LazyLayoutCacheWindow graduates to stable across LazyColumn, LazyRow, LazyVerticalGrid, and staggered grids, moving cache window configuration directly onto the layout composable rather than burying it inside rememberLazyListState. It replaces legacy single-item prefetching with a viewport-relative ahead-and-behind window. At the same time, FlexBox and Grid graduate to stable while FlowRow, FlowColumn, ConstraintLayout, and MotionLayout in Compose are deprecated in favor of FlexBox:

kotlin import androidx.compose.foundation.layout.FlexBox import androidx.compose.foundation.lazy.LazyColumn import androidx.compose.foundation.lazy.LazyLayoutCacheWindow import androidx.compose.foundation.lazy.items import androidx.compose.runtime.Composable import androidx.compose.ui.unit.dp

kotlin @Composable fun TagCatalogList(sections: CatalogSectionList) { // Retain half a viewport behind and prefetch one full viewport ahead val cacheWindow = LazyLayoutCacheWindow( aheadFraction = 1.0f, behindFraction = 0.5f )

kotlin LazyColumn(cacheWindow = cacheWindow) { items(sections, key = { it.id }) { section -> // Stable FlexBox replaces FlowRow and limits wrapped lines to 4 items max FlexBox(maxItemsInEachLine = 4) { section.tags.forEach { tag -> TagChip(label = tag) } } } } }

Two smaller Compose 1.13 changes also show up in profiling traces:

Once your code compiles cleanly against BOM 2026.11.01, you can wire Android CLI 1.1.16919734 into local pre-commit hooks or CI pipelines to verify UI layout trees, test interactive flows, and guard against APK size regressions without booting a desktop IDE.

Diagram showing android preview render, android layout and input JSON output, and android signals Crashlytics pipeline

With android preview, you can enumerate every @Preview composable in a module and render both its visual PNG output and its semantic component tree JSON straight from the command line:

android preview render \ --fqn com.example.telemetryapp.ui.OrderCardPreview \ --output artifacts/previews/

The command writes both the rendered .png image and the structured component tree .json file into ./artifacts/previews/, making visual regression diffs and accessibility tree checks straightforward in headless Linux CI containers.

In Android CLI 1.1.16919734, two important reliability fixes landed for automation scripts:

Combine android emulator start --headless, android layout, and android input to run a smoke check from a shell script:

android emulator start Pixel_9_API_37 --headless

android layout --no-idle > artifacts/current-layout.json

android input tap "text=Confirm Order" | jq -e '.results[0].status == "OK"'

For instrumentation tests running via runComposeUiTest, pair your CLI pipeline with Compose 1.13's new TestFailurePolicy. Whenever an assertion fails in CI, TestFailurePolicy automatically dumps a screenshot and the full UI semantics hierarchy at the exact instant of failure:

kotlin import androidx.compose.ui.test.ComposeUiTestConfig import androidx.compose.ui.test.ExperimentalTestApi import androidx.compose.ui.test.FailureArtifact import androidx.compose.ui.test.TestFailureHandler import androidx.compose.ui.test.TestFailurePolicy import androidx.compose.ui.test.runComposeUiTest import org.junit.Test

kotlin @OptIn(ExperimentalTestApi::class) private val ciFailureConfig = ComposeUiTestConfig( failurePolicy = TestFailurePolicy( screenshotCaptureMode = TestFailurePolicy.CaptureMode.Enabled, uiHierarchyCaptureMode = TestFailurePolicy.CaptureMode.Enabled, failureHandlers = listOf( TestFailureHandler { context -> val screenshot = context.artifacts.firstOrNull { it.type == FailureArtifact.Type.Screenshot } println("Captured failure artifact: ${screenshot?.name} for ${context.error.message}") } ) ) )

kotlin @OptIn(ExperimentalTestApi::class) @test fun confirmOrderDisplaysReceipt() = runComposeUiTest(config = ciFailureConfig) { // Test assertions here automatically dump PNG + semantics tree on failure } }

You can also turn on both captures globally across an entire test suite without touching individual test classes by adding testInstrumentationRunnerArguments in build.gradle.kts:

gradle android { defaultConfig { testInstrumentationRunnerArguments["androidx.compose.ui.test.failure.isScreenshotCaptureEnabled"] = "true" testInstrumentationRunnerArguments["androidx.compose.ui.test.failure.isUiHierarchyCaptureEnabled"] = "true" } }

Before publishing a release build or an internal library AAR, run android analyze --format=json to inspect DEX footprints, R8 shrinking metrics, and warnings about overly broad consumer keep rules. Note a breaking schema change in Android CLI 1.1.16919734: for library artifacts (.aar and .jar), the dexSizeBytes fields inside d8CompileResult and r8CompileResult are now serialized as 64-bit integer strings rather than JSON numbers.

Update any jq or Python CI scripts that parse android analyze output to convert dexSizeBytes with tonumber:

android analyze --format=json library/build/outputs/aar/library-release.aar \ | jq '.r8CompileResult.dexSizeBytes | tonumber'

When an issue escapes staging and shows up in production, Android CLI's android signals command connects directly to Firebase Crashlytics so you can inspect crash clusters and download field out-of-memory (OOM) heap dumps without leaving your terminal:

android signals crashes list --app com.example.telemetryapp android signals crashes facets --issue-id CRASH_ISSUE_ID android signals crashes samples --issue-id CRASH_ISSUE_ID

android signals heaps list --app com.example.telemetryapp android signals heaps download --heap-id HEAP_ID --output traces/field-oom.hprof

Pulling a field heap dump alongside the session's custom keys and logs lets you compare real-world memory retention directly against the local --type=perfetto heap dumps you captured during development.

When upgrading a team repository across Android CLI 1.1.16919734, SDK Platform-Tools 37.0.3, and Compose BOM 2026.11.01 simultaneously, most build or script failures trace back to one of six breaking changes.

The table below lists the exact symptom, root cause, and fix for each issue.

By moving your local shell and CI pipelines onto Android CLI 1.1.16919734, SDK Platform-Tools 37.0.3, and Jetpack Compose 1.13, you cut out redundant APK file uploads, stabilize USB and wireless adb connections, and turn Compose recomposition hunting from visual guesswork into repeatable command-line traces.

If You Run Another Round of Promotion and It Still Stalls, Where Do You Go From There?

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Four weeks of promotion. Six short videos, zero views. UTM-tagged social links, zero recorded sign-ups. Yet in week four alone, the email list grew by 24 people.

You've run a campaign, pulled in some traffic, and gotten a few replies, but revenue hasn't budged. Many teams' first instinct is to publish more content or buy more traffic—figuring a customer will turn up eventually.

On the surface, you're deciding whether to run another push. In reality, you're stuck between two very different questions:

1.Did the last round actually reach people who need this product and help them get it?

2.If they did get it, why didn't they take the next step?

Until you separate those two questions, your next move is likely to miss. If you haven't answered the first, pulling the plug on promotion might be too early. If you already have clues on the second, spending more to widen reach can easily send money to the wrong place. For the founder in the original post, the visible cost was hours of work. For a small team about to pay for ads on the next round, it's straight cash out the door.

As long as attribution stays broken, though, another round will likely hand them another report they can't really read.

If your numbers look that murky, hold off on scaling up time or ad spend for a moment. Before buying another wave of reach, test "did it land" and "does it compel" separately. The most direct way is to put your last campaign in front of a few people who have this exact problem right now:

● If they get it and are willing to test it on real work, the core message holds up. Next, it's worth checking whether your usual channels can bring in more people like them.

● If several people get it and nobody wants to try, that's already enough reason to pause paid reach—put the time you would have spent buying impressions toward learning how they handle the problem today, and why switching feels worth it or not.

A handful of conversations won't solve distribution on their own, but they help you pick which thread to pull first.

Sometimes, though, the blocker is already sitting in plain sight—no extra campaign needed to guess.

On Hacker News, a founder building a website tool for small businesses shared that after three months of promotion and plenty of positive feedback, only one customer had paid—and later canceled. A commenter who looked at the site said that what looked like a requirement to connect Facebook put them off right away, since they didn't want to link an account and couldn't tell what data would be read.

That's a concrete drop-off signal. If people who actually want a website keep bailing at that step, buying more traffic before clearing up permission concerns just pushes more visitors into the same dead end. Fixing that friction has far stronger backing than buying another round of reach.

Stagnation isn't a cue to move on by default; it's a business signal that needs an explanation. When promotion doesn't click, you shouldn't keep pouring money in out of sunk cost, nor write off the product over a flat stretch—you can pause the ad spend and let the next piece of evidence decide where the product goes. Separating "is this campaign worth funding" from "is this business worth running" is how you read a real signal instead of gambling on luck.

So before you put down another check or sink another few weeks of hours into promotion, ask yourself one question:

"Even if this next round brings zero orders, what specific thing will I be able to see—and how will that change where I put my next dollar or hour?"

If the last round already paid off on its own terms, running it again as-is makes sense. If the return still isn't there and your current numbers can't explain why, pausing blind spend and putting your energy on the clearest bottleneck right now beats charging ahead in the dark.

Dow Reclaims 50,000 as Inflation and GDP Data Land Before Thanksgiving

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

NEW YORK, Nov. 25, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Market Updates News Commentary - Wall Street heads into the busiest morning of a holiday-shortened week with the Dow Jones Industrial Average back above 50,000. The blue-chip index added 423.39 points on Tuesday to close at 50,018.70, while the Nasdaq composite fell 356.02 points to 23,733.54 a day after its Monday surge. According to AP, the S&P 500 slipped 36.08 points to 7,201.09 even though most of the stocks within the index climbed. Active companies from around the markets with current developments this week include: Merck & Co., Inc. (NYSE: MRK), Workday, Inc. (NASDAQ: WDAY), Deere & Company (NYSE: DE).

The week opened with a sharp rebound. On Monday, the S&P 500 rallied 326.78 points to 7,237.17, the Nasdaq composite jumped 1,471.82 points to 24,089.56 and the Dow gained 1,447.98 points to 49,595.31. AP described it as the S&P 500's best day since April of 2025. Even after Tuesday's mixed finish, the Dow is up 3.9% for the week so far and the S&P 500 is up 4.2%.

Consumers brought good news of their own. The Conference Board said its Consumer Confidence Index rose by 11.4 points to 103.3 in November, up from 91.9 in October. "Consumer confidence rallied again in November," said Fergus McCormick, Senior Economist, Global Indicators, The Conference Board.

The economic calendar is packed into a single morning. The Bureau of Economic Analysis is scheduled to release Personal Income and Outlays for October 2026, which includes the PCE price index, along with the second estimate of third-quarter GDP and corporate profits at 8:30 a.m. ET, according to its release schedule. The Census Bureau's calendar lists the advance report on durable goods at 8:30 a.m. ET and new residential sales at 10:00 a.m. ET.

After that, the holiday takes over. U.S. stock markets are closed on Thursday, November 26 for Thanksgiving, and the NYSE says each market will close early at 1:00 p.m. ET on Friday, November 27.

Merck & Co., Inc. (NYSE: MRK) reported third-quarter 2026 results on October 29, with total worldwide sales of $18.1 billion, representing 5% growth and 4% growth excluding foreign exchange, GAAP EPS of $2.04 and non-GAAP EPS of $2.49. The company now anticipates full-year 2026 sales to be between $66.8 billion and $67.3 billion. On November 20, Merck announced that the pivotal Phase 3 TroFuse-015 trial evaluating sacituzumab tirumotecan, an investigational TROP2-directed antibody-drug conjugate, met its primary endpoint of overall survival.

"Our science-led strategy is transforming our business," said Robert M. Davis, Chairman and Chief Executive Officer of Merck.

Workday, Inc. (NASDAQ: WDAY) reported fiscal 2027 third-quarter results on Tuesday. Total revenues were $2.705 billion, an increase of 11.2% from the same period last year, and subscription revenues were $2.532 billion, up 12.8%. Its 12-month subscription revenue backlog was $9.216 billion, up 12.3%, and the company updated its full-year guidance to subscription revenues of $9.958 billion, representing growth of 13%, from a prior range of $9.940 billion to $9.950 billion.

"The past few quarters have been our strongest on the innovation and invention front, and in Q3, our AI products drove nearly $150 million in new ACV, more than three times what we delivered a year ago," said Aneel Bhusri, Co-founder, CEO, and Chair, Workday.

Deere & Company (NYSE: DE) will hold its fourth quarter 2026 earnings call on Wednesday, November 25, beginning at 9:00 a.m. central time. In its third-quarter release, Deere reported worldwide net sales and revenues up 5 percent to $12.608 billion and forecast fiscal 2026 net income attributable to Deere & Company in a range of $4.75 billion to $5.00 billion.

"Deere delivered a strong quarter, reflecting disciplined execution by our teams and continued resilience across our portfolio," said John C. May, Chairman and Chief Executive Officer of Deere and Company.

With confidence on the rise, a fresh batch of inflation and growth data due before the opening bell and only a day and a half of trading left in the week, Wednesday's session sets the tone for the Thanksgiving break.

Wishpond Reports Q3-2026 Financial Results and Announces Board Update

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

VANCOUVER, BC, Nov. 25, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Wishpond Technologies Ltd. (TSXV: WISH) (OTCQX: WPNDF) (the "Company" or "Wishpond"), a provider of AI-enabled marketing-focused online business solutions, announces that it has filed its interim consolidated financial statements (the "Interim Financial Statements") and management's discussion and analysis (the "MD&A") for Q3-2026, representing the three and nine months ended September 30, 2026. Copies of the Interim Financial Statements and MD&A are available on the Company's profile on SEDAR+ at www.sedarplus.ca. Further, Wishpond announces that Olivier Vincent has stepped down from the Company's Board of Directors, effective November 17, 2026.

Jordan Gutierrez, Wishpond's Chief Executive Officer, commented, "In the third quarter we continued to focus Wishpond on its core marketing technology platform and to align the Company's cost structure with the size of the business. Gross Margin(1) improved from the second quarter, and we remain focused on the core platform and our customers. I also wish to express my thanks and appreciation to Olivier, on behalf of the Board and management, for his many contributions to Wishpond over the past six years. We all wish him success in his future endeavors."

Jacky He, Wishpond's Chief Financial Officer, commented, "Gross Margin was 69% in the third quarter, up from 66% in the second quarter (Q3-2025: 71%). Our priorities for the remainder of 2026 are a leaner operating base, improved retention in the core platform and disciplined management of liquidity, including continuing to work with our senior lender. Wishpond shareholders also continue to participate in the progress of SalesCloser Technologies Ltd. ("SalesCloser") through the Company's approximately 62.87% interest in SalesCloser."

For 2026, Wishpond is focused on strengthening its core marketing technology platform and sales solutions business, with an emphasis on its core Wishpond marketing suite, improving operating efficiency and enhancing financial flexibility. Following the completion of the Viral Loops divestiture and the SalesCloser transaction earlier in 2026, the Company is focused on executing against its core business and aligning resources around the products, services and customer relationships that management believes are most central to Wishpond's long-term operating performance.

The Company has taken steps in 2025 and 2026 to streamline operations and align its cost structure more closely with revenue levels, and management intends to continue those efforts through the remainder of 2026. Wishpond remains focused on supporting the performance of its core platform and product suite, improving customer acquisition and retention and continuing to develop AI-enabled capabilities intended to enhance the effectiveness of its marketing and sales solutions.

Following the completion of the SalesCloser transaction, Wishpond continues to maintain exposure to SalesCloser through its 62.87% ownership interest as at September 30, 2026, while management remains focused on executing against the Company's core marketing technology business. Jordan Gutierrez was appointed Chief Executive Officer of Wishpond effective March 26, 2026, and the Company enters the remainder of 2026 with a more focused operating structure and clearer strategic priorities.

Wishpond is a Vancouver-based provider of AI-enabled marketing and sales solutions that help businesses grow more efficiently. The Company's vision is to create a fully autonomous AI-enabled platform that streamlines the entire customer acquisition journey, from lead generation and engagement to deal closure, enabling businesses to scale cost-effectively while driving higher conversions. Wishpond offers an all-in-one marketing suite that integrates AI-driven tools such as an AI website builder and AI email automation. The Company serves small-to-medium-sized businesses across various industries, providing a cost-effective alternative to fragmented marketing solutions. Wishpond employs a software-as-a-service (SaaS) business model, generating most of its revenue from subscription-based recurring revenue, which supports revenue predictability and cash flow visibility while continuously expanding its AI capabilities. Wishpond is listed on the TSXV under the ticker "WISH", and on the OTCQX Best Market under the ticker "WPNDF". For further information, visit: www.wishpond.com.

Fifth win in the category since 2019 and second year running

LONDON, Nov. 25, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- CloudMargin, the world's first and only cloudânative collateral and margin management solution, has won Collateral Management and Optimisation Product of the Year at Risk.net's Markets Technology Awards 2027. Presented at a dinner ceremony in London last night, the award is CloudMargin's fifth win in the category since 2019 and its second consecutive year earning the honour.

A judging panel of technology users and Risk.net editors choose the winners, on the strength of entries alone.

The win follows another year of progress across several fronts, according to CloudMargin CEO Stuart Connolly. CloudMargin now supports tokenised securities, tokenised money market funds (MMFs) and stablecoins, alongside traditional securities and cash. The platform's futures commission merchant (FCM) support and exchangeâtraded derivatives (ETD) capabilities have expanded further, and the team has laid the groundwork for agentic AI within the platform.

Connolly said: "Markets are moving faster than ever, and firms need realâtime visibility and control to keep pace. Over the past year, we've made CloudMargin digitalâasset ready, giving clients a broader, more mobile collateral pool. Next on our product roadmap is using agentic AI to drive higher levels of automation and facilitating clients' broader agentic journey. We're delighted to win this award for the second year running, demonstrating what a modern, cloudânative platform makes possible."

Clients relied on CloudMargin through the heightened volatility of the first half of 2026. Since the start of the year, the platform has processed more than 1.64 million margin calls and more than 1.6 million collateral bookings, scaling seamlessly via its AWS cloud-native architecture. With over 260 leading buyâside and sellâside clients, CloudMargin continues to grow strongly year on year.

Headquartered in London, CloudMargin is the blueprint for modern collateral management technology as the world's first and only cloudânative collateral and margin management solution. Its awardâwinning platform transforms collateral management workflows across the banking, buyâside and outsourcing industries, combining automation, straightâthrough processing (STP), realâtime analytics and ecosystem connectivity. Trusted by over 260 organisations globally, CloudMargin unifies collateral and liquidity management into one intelligent, purposeâbuilt collateral management platform to automate workflows endâtoâend, eliminate silos, optimise inventory in realâtime and reduce overhead. Student Photographer Who Lost Right Eye While Covering LA Protest Sues Federal Government

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Student Photographer Who Lost Right Eye While Covering LA Protest Sues Federal Government

A student photographer, who lost his right eye after he was allegedly struck by a projectile fired by a Department of Homeland Security (DHS) agent while covering a “No Kings” protest, is suing the federal government.

Warning: The following photos contain content that some may find upsetting.

Tucker Collins, an 18-year-old freshman at the University of Southern California (USC), was left partially blind after photographing demonstrators in downtown Los Angeles on the afternoon of March 28. Collins was allegedly photographing a confrontation between protesters and federal agents when he was allegedly hit by a less-lethal projectile.

Footage shows Collins taking pictures of demonstrators outside a federal facility before suddenly falling to his knees as a crowd-control projectile reportedly struck him in the right eye, fracturing bones in his eye socket. Collins was taken to a hospital, where he underwent two and a half hours of surgery in an attempt to save the eye. The injury was too severe, and the eye was surgically removed.

Collins sued the federal government on Monday, alleging that law enforcement officers used excessive force and violated his civil rights. According to a report by The New York Times, his attorneys allege that Department of Homeland Security (DHS) officers “assaulted” and “used unjustified force against him” as he photographed the protest.

The lawsuit seeks various damages from the federal government, “including past and future medical expenses, prosthetic and ophthalmologic care, educational expenses, and loss of earning capacity.”

Collins’s attorney, V. James DeSimone, and his family are also demanding criminal charges against the officer responsible for shooting him. They argue that the officer’s actions violated California law governing the use of projectile launchers by law enforcement.

The lawsuit also details how Collins’s injuries have limited his ability to pursue activities he once enjoyed, including photography. Collins says he is no longer able to safely photograph public events similar to the protest where he was injured. DeSimone also condemned the lack of criminal accountability in the wake of the shooting.

“It is deeply disturbing that the maiming of Tucker Collins, resulting in the loss of his eye, was widely reported and captured on video and, yet, there is no law enforcement response, to date, on the Federal, State or Local level to prosecute the offending officer for his crime,” DeSimone says in a statement. “Once again, it is left to civil rights attorneys to attempt to achieve some semblance of justice for the permanent and irreversible harm caused to this courageous young man.”
